and supported by anesthesiologists who provide advice and personally assess the more complex patients.<sup>99</sup> In the United States, anesthesiologist- led, protocol- driven preoperative assessment is often used for healthy patients having minor procedures. However, these preoperative evaluation clinics are frequently used in US hospitals, not just for ambulatory patients with more complex medical or surgical issues, but also for the majority of inpatient surgery cases that are admitted on the morning of surgery. More complex patients have their anesthesia preassessment performed by an anesthesiologist. A comprehensive preoperative history and physical evaluation by a physician extender is often also provided in the preoperative assessment clinic for the surgeon. Using a physician extender to assist in preoperative evaluation maintains patient safety and satisfaction, promotes flexibility with scheduling of providers, and increases staff satisfaction.<sup>100</sup> Appropriately trained nurses were just as effective as trainee medical staff in detecting information likely to influence subsequent patient management, but they ordered significantly fewer unnecessary tests.<sup>101</sup>

Patients generally rate their preoperative assessment clinic experience very favorably, with their greatest concerns relating to waiting times.<sup>102</sup> Scheduling appointments of approximately twice as long for patients of ASA physical status grades III and IV than for those of grades I and II has been shown to reduce backlogs and maximum waiting times for preoperative evaluation to an acceptable level of about 10 minutes.<sup>103</sup>

### PREOPERATIVE INVESTIGATION

The history and physical examination remain key elements of preoperative risk assessment, despite the availability of more sophisticated technologies.<sup>81</sup> In fact, most useful information can be obtained from the history, supplemented by simple observation of the patient.<sup>104</sup> Basic physical examination, such as routine chest auscultation, is often considered unhelpful in adult ambulatory surgery patients,<sup>93,105</sup> because findings that are unaccompanied by symptoms or functional limitation do not alter management. Although aortic stenosis may remain asymptomatic until it is quite severe, chest auscultation may be unreliable in its detection. In one high- risk population, 31% of patients without a detectable murmur nevertheless had some degree of aortic stenosis that was moderate or severe in 10% of cases,<sup>106</sup> whereas 31% of patients with a suggestive murmur did not have aortic stenosis on echocardiography. Ten patients with severe aortic stenosis (valve area 1 cm<sup>2</sup> or less, gradient 35–58 mm Hg) tolerated an unmodified anesthesia regimen for electroconvulsive therapy on 144 occasions without problems,<sup>107</sup> suggesting that ambulatory surgical patients with undiagnosed aortic stenosis may not inevitably be at excessive risk, when combined with appropriate procedure selection.

It is recognized that routine laboratory investigations are unhelpful because they may generate false- positive results or do not alter subsequent management.<sup>108</sup> In addition, such

<!-- PDF page 9 -->

tests increase costs, are unpleasant and time- consuming for patients, and may lead to repeat testing with even further expense and delays. Consequently, many authorities recommend selective testing, based on indications from the patient’s clinical evaluation and demographics.<sup>109,110</sup>

Although comorbidities become more prevalent with age, additional preoperative testing for older patients may still be unnecessary. In patients 70 years of age or older, routine preoperative blood test results were not predictive of postoperative complications.<sup>111</sup> While preoperative electrocardiograms of patients over 50 years of age detected abnormalities, such as bundle branch blocks, predictive of postoperative myocardial infarction, the recordings did not provide additional predictive value to that obtained from the patient history.<sup>112</sup> The National Institute for Health and Care Excellence no longer advocates patient age be used as a criteria for routine preoperative testing and also recommends few, if any, tests for healthy patients undergoing minor or intermediate surgery.<sup>110</sup> One large pilot study demonstrated that the elimination of all preoperative testing resulted in no increase in adverse events in the perioperative period and did not alter the rate of unanticipated admission or readmission within 30 days compared to indicated testing.<sup>113</sup>

## Patient Preparation

Preoperative assessment plays a fundamental role in preparing patients for their ambulatory surgery experience. This may include ensuring appropriate social support is in place, checking that preexisting conditions are optimally treated, and providing information.

### PROVISION OF INFORMATION

Patients need to be informed about what will happen on the day of surgery, because a well- prepared patient is likely to be more relaxed and more satisfied with the service provided.<sup>93</sup> A well- informed patient is also more likely to comply with important instructions and protocols, such as fasting intervals and management of regular medications. Many patients are disproportionately worried about highly unlikely outcomes, such as death and awareness,<sup>114</sup> yet somewhat less concerned about more common consequences such as nausea, vomiting, and postoperative discomfort. The preoperative discussion should help to allay the anxiety associated with unlikely risks. It was found that a specially designed website with text, animation, and video significantly increased patients’ knowledge about anesthesia when compared with a combination of verbal and written information.<sup>115</sup>

Patients should be given specific information, preferably supplemented in writing, about how to manage their usual medications in the preoperative period. Some drugs (e.g., warfarin) should be stopped several days before surgery<sup>86</sup> and some hypoglycemic agents omitted before surgery.<sup>116,117</sup> However, other important medications should not be omitted.<sup>78,79,88</sup>

Information about medications should include over- thecounter and herbal products, which are commonly used but often regarded by patients as harmless and safe.<sup>118</sup> Despite some serious consequences and interactions associated

with herbal medications,<sup>119</sup> not all practices specifically ask and advise patients about these products on a routine basis (see also Chapter 28).<sup>120</sup>

### PREOPERATIVE FASTING

Almost universal agreement now exists that the safe fasting interval for clear liquids is no more than 2 hours, in contrast to 6 hours for light meals.<sup>121,122</sup> These fasting intervals result in safe gastric volumes even in obese adults,<sup>123</sup> children,<sup>124</sup> and patients with diabetes and gastrointestinal (GI) reflux.<sup>122</sup> Indeed, a 2- hour interval is probably conservative, given that the stomach empties clear fluids exponentially, with a half- time of approximately 10 minutes.<sup>125</sup>

Despite guidelines based on decades of research, implementation remains poor, with many patients fasting for excessive periods and experiencing significant discomfort.<sup>126–128</sup> Rather than focusing on a minimum fasting time, patients should be encouraged to keep drinking until the latest time necessary for safety to reduce preoperative dehydration and its associated consequences.<sup>122</sup> In practice, this may mean asking patients to have a drink just before they leave home or even providing a drink on arrival if surgery is still more than 2 hours away. Advising patients to drink on the morning of surgery also makes it easier for them to take their medications. Milk added to tea or coffee does not appear to delay gastric emptying<sup>129–131</sup> and, in the United Kingdom, may provide more palatable choices of preoperative beverage. The US practice is for clear fluids only. Recently, it has been shown that allowing patients unrestricted access to water right up until they were called to the operating room significantly reduced the incidence of postoperative nausea and vomiting (PONV) without increasing the occurrence of clinically significant pulmonary aspiration.<sup>132</sup> Practices such as chewing gum before surgery also may not be harmful. No consistent evidence exists of a clinically important increase in gastric volume associated with gum chewing in adults,<sup>122,133</sup> while in children it may promote gastric emptying and could also serve as a useful route for premedication.<sup>134</sup>

Besides feelings of thirst and hunger, excessive fasting also results in hypoglycemia in significant numbers of patients, with 14% of fasted healthy female ambulatory surgical patients having an admission blood glucose value of 45 mg/ dL (2.5 mmol/L) or less.<sup>135</sup> Preoperative oral carbohydrates have been shown to improve subjective well- being, reduce thirst and hunger, and reduce postoperative insulin resistance, although convincing evidence of reduced length of stay is lacking,<sup>122</sup> at least for shorter procedures.<sup>136</sup>

### PREMEDICATION

Premedication traditionally refers to the administration of medications to relieve anxiety before surgery. However, the term encompasses any medication given in the preoperative period and therefore includes prophylactic analgesia and antiemesis agents, as well as drugs used to promote gastric emptying or counteract acid secretions.

### Management of Anxiety

The use of anxiolytic premedication is now uncommon in ambulatory anesthesia,<sup>137,138</sup> probably because of

<!-- PDF page 10 -->

concerns that these medications will delay recovery. In fact, one meta- analysis found no evidence that anxiolytic premedication delayed the discharge of ambulatory patients, although impaired performance in some tests of psychomotor function was found and the authors questioned the relevance of some of the older studies to modern ambulatory practice now that short- acting anesthetics are the norm.<sup>139</sup>

Anxiety is nevertheless common in ambulatory surgery patients,<sup>137</sup> with up to two- thirds showing symptoms. A preoperative consultation more than 2 weeks before surgery can reduce patient anxiety and improve satisfaction, especially if the anesthesiologist was perceived as empathic.<sup>140</sup> Patient satisfaction was further improved when intraoperative care was provided by the same anesthesiologist who performed the preoperative consultation.<sup>140</sup>

### Anxiolytic Premedication

Given that anxiety was commonly reported, doubtless some patients will benefit from anxiolytic premedication, but what is the optimal regimen? Oral midazolam provided more anxiolysis than temazepam but also produced more sedation and amnesia, resulted in more oversedated patients, and delayed recovery.<sup>141</sup> Oral alprazolam produced comparable anxiety reduction to midazolam without causing amnesia,<sup>142</sup> but it also caused greater impairment of psychomotor function in the early postoperative period. Neither anxiolytic premedication delayed clinical recovery, but this may be a relatively crude assessment tool, because recovery primarily depends on other factors. Intravenous midazolam given shortly before induction of anesthesia reduced both anxiety and postoperative nausea.<sup>143</sup> Such relatively late administration will not reduce anxiety while the patient waits preoperatively, but may be useful in a patient who needs to have an uncomfortable procedure before surgery, such as mammographic needle localization, in which satisfaction with both the procedure and subsequent breast biopsy was improved.<sup>144</sup>

Premedication is more commonly used in children. In one investigation, premedication with oral midazolam 0.2 mg/kg reduced emergence agitation associated with sevoflurane anesthesia without significant delay in recovery,<sup>145</sup> and doses up to 0.5 mg/kg did not delay recovery.<sup>146</sup> However, other patients have experienced delayed recovery at this dose<sup>147,148</sup> while not always having a reduction in anxiety.<sup>148</sup> The less stringent recovery end points needed for children compared with the independent functioning needed for adults should be kept in mind. Premedication with oral midazolam may itself provoke anxiety in children, but giving the child a small toy to play with first has been shown to be effective in reducing this anxiety.<sup>149</sup> Unfortunately, the effect of the toy as an alternative to midazolam was not evaluated. Play therapy and distraction can be more effective than midazolam in reducing anxiety in children, although this may require an elaborate approach.<sup>150</sup> A preoperative educational program portrayed as a “Saturday morning club” also reduced anxiety,<sup>151</sup> although the authors questioned whether the benefits achieved were worth the time and resources required. A simpler and highly effective approach is to allow children to watch ageappropriate video clips or movies during anesthetic induction with intravenous (South Korea) or inhaled (Canada) techniques.<sup>152,153</sup> A preoperative educational video also

encouraged more positive parental involvement during the recovery period and reduced children’s postoperative pain.<sup>154</sup>

Because of variable efficacy and the potential for delayed recovery, alternatives to benzodiazepine premedication have been sought. In children, oral transmucosal fentanyl reduced preoperative anxiety and postoperative agitation, but a high incidence of predictable side effects, such as PONV and delayed discharge, limited its usefulness.<sup>155</sup> Selective α2- adrenergic agonists have potentially useful sedative and analgesic effects, yet despite years of study they still have no clearly defined role in ambulatory surgery where the risks for adverse events outweigh their advantages.<sup>156</sup> Clonidine is commonly used in pediatric practice, particularly to reduce agitation during emergence,<sup>157</sup> but the promising results of clinical trials do not always translate well into routine clinical practice and its effects on induction anxiety are less persuasive.<sup>158</sup>

### Analgesic Premedication

Ambulatory patients are commonly premedicated with prophylactic oral nonopioid analgesia in the expectation of achieving analgesic levels in the early postoperative period. Because the duration of action of acetaminophen (paracetamol) is relatively short (4–6 hours), it is unlikely to provide worthwhile postoperative analgesia, other than for an extremely short procedure. When patients received oral acetaminophen 1 g an hour before arthroscopic knee surgery, barely a third had plasma levels in the therapeutic analgesic range 30 minutes after arriving in recovery, whereas therapeutic levels were consistently achieved with intraoperative intravenous administration.<sup>159</sup>

The nonsteroidal anti- inflammatory drugs (NSAIDs) are consistently more effective as analgesic premedications. Some evidence indicates that NSAIDs have a small preemptive analgesic effect (i.e., they achieve a superior effect if given before rather than after surgery),<sup>160,161</sup> although one of the studies most strongly supporting this conclusion has been called into question.<sup>162</sup> Preoperative parecoxib followed by postoperative valdecoxib significantly reduced the opioid analgesic requirements and the incidence of opioid- related adverse effects after laparoscopic cholecystectomy.<sup>163</sup> As well as providing effective postoperative analgesia, etoricoxib premedication had an anestheticsparing effect during ambulatory ankle surgery.<sup>164</sup> Using a common dental model of postoperative pain, preoperative ibuprofen, diclofenac, and acetaminophen with codeine effectively controlled early postoperative pain.<sup>165</sup> Similarly, rofecoxib and ketorolac were equally effective in controlling postoperative pain after ambulatory surgery,<sup>166</sup> as were ibuprofen and ketorolac.<sup>167</sup> Sustained- release preparations of NSAIDs improve convenience by allowing earlier preoperative administration and a prolonged postoperative effect. Modified- release ibuprofen 1.6 g delayed the time until rescue analgesia was required after ambulatory third molar surgery in contrast to standard ibuprofen.<sup>168</sup> Several of these NSAIDs and most cyclooxygenase- 2 (COX- 2) inhibitors are unavailable in the United States.

Selective COX- 2 inhibitors are no more effective and offer few advantages over traditional NSAIDs in ambulatory surgery. Despite any inhibition of platelet function, the amount of blood loss seen during high- risk surgery,

<!-- PDF page 11 -->

such as tonsillectomy, was not reduced in comparison with nonselective NSAIDs.<sup>169,170</sup> Nonetheless, COX- 2 inhibitors are preferred by surgeons, so age- appropriate doses of oral celecoxib are used as premedication in the United States. Attempts to limit GI adverse effects, which are rare in acute use, has introduced other side effects and led to some drugs being withdrawn.<sup>171</sup> Selective COX- 2 inhibitors may be better tolerated in patients with aspirin- sensitive asthma.<sup>172</sup>

Several other drugs have been evaluated for premedication without strong evidence for this use. In combination with ibuprofen, pregabalin 150 mg reduced median pain scores at rest and in motion after gynecologic laparoscopic surgery, but did not reduce postoperative analgesic requirements.<sup>173</sup> Perioperative administration of pregabalin 75 mg provided short- lived pain reduction after laparoscopic cholecystectomy, and a meta- analysis confirmed limited analgesic benefit, no reduction in opioid side effects, and increased sedation from its use.<sup>174</sup> Pretreatment with magnesium sulfate 4 g had no impact on postoperative pain or analgesic consumption in patients undergoing ambulatory ilioinguinal hernia repair or varicose vein surgery.<sup>175</sup>

### Antacid and Gastrokinetic Premedication

The incidence of aspiration of gastric contents is infrequent in fasted elective surgical patients. Despite improvements in several surrogate measures, insufficient evidence exists of clinical benefit (i.e., a reduction in morbidity or mortality from aspiration) to recommend the routine use of antacids, metoclopramide, H2- receptor antagonists, or proton pump inhibitors before elective ambulatory surgery.<sup>121,122</sup> Patients who are receiving these medications chronically should take them before surgery. Patients who regularly suffer from significant acid reflux in the fasted state will also benefit from the head- up tilt position during induction of anesthesia. This approach also may be useful in the supermorbidly obese patient undergoing bariatric surgery, in whom the use of prophylactic proton pump inhibitors and sodium citrate may be considered.<sup>55</sup>

## Anesthetic Techniques

### CHOICE OF TECHNIQUE

Ambulatory surgery requires the same basic standards as inpatient surgery in terms of facilities, staffing, and provision of equipment for delivery of anesthetic drugs, monitoring, and resuscitation. Quality, safety, efficiency, and the cost of drugs and equipment are all important considerations in choosing an anesthetic technique for ambulatory surgery. The choice of specific anesthetic drugs or techniques should enable appropriate and controllable intraoperative conditions followed by rapid recovery with minimal side effects and a prompt return to normal psychomotor activity. Achieving these aims also requires careful attention to the details of analgesia, antiemesis, and fluid management, and requires experienced staff to deliver a high- quality, efficient, and cost- effective service.

No single ideal anesthetic drug or technique for ambulatory surgery exists, and the choice depends on both surgical and patient factors. General anesthesia commonly

remains the most popular technique with both patients and surgeons, in some cases despite clear benefits of local or regional anesthetic techniques.<sup>176</sup> Spinal anesthesia can be useful for lower extremity and perineal procedures, but a low bupivacaine dose may be needed in the ambulatory setting<sup>177,178</sup> to prevent delays in discharge because of residual motor or sympathetic block. Thus, prilocaine, chloroprocaine, and mepivacaine are receiving new attention for ambulatory surgery.<sup>179–181</sup> Infiltration of local anesthesia, peripheral nerve blocks, or both facilitate the recovery process by reducing postoperative pain and minimizing the need for opioid analgesics after general anesthesia and should be used whenever possible. Many ambulatory procedures also can be performed using local anesthetic techniques, supplemented, if necessary, with sedatives, analgesic drugs, or both.

### GENERAL ANESTHESIA

Induction of general anesthesia is typically achieved with a rapid and short- acting intravenous anesthetic, although inhaled induction is often used in children and needle- phobic adults. Intravenous drugs are also popular for maintenance of anesthesia, especially where the availability of targetcontrolled infusion (TCI) systems simplifies delivery,<sup>182</sup> but the use of short- acting inhaled anesthetics, usually without nitrous oxide (N2O), is more common due to greater ease of administration and lower risk of intraoperative awareness. No single anesthetic technique is clearly superior for ambulatory surgery. Other factors, such as the experience of the anesthesiologist, the use of adjuvant drugs, and the art of anesthesia, are still important to deliver the best quality care.<sup>183</sup>

### INTRAVENOUS ANESTHESIA

Barbiturates, such as methohexital and thiopental, are of little more than historical interest in ambulatory anesthesia. The same applies to benzodiazepines and ketamine when used at the doses needed to induce general anesthesia. Etomidate is associated with myoclonus, pain on injection, and a high incidence of PONV. Although reformulation of etomidate in a lipid solvent can reduce some of these disadvantages,<sup>184</sup> persisting concerns about suppression of adrenal steroidogenesis<sup>185</sup> have curtailed its use. The search for new and improved intravenous anesthetic drugs has seen several promising compounds evaluated,<sup>186,187</sup> but no really novel anesthetics have yet become available<sup>188</sup> and propofol remains the most practical intravenous anesthetic for ambulatory anesthesia.

### Propofol

Propofol has many of the properties of an ideal anesthetic induction drug and is popular because it rapidly and smoothly induces anesthesia without airway irritation and results in a rapid recovery with an infrequent incidence of early PONV<sup>189</sup> and a “clear head.” Propofol has some disadvantages, however, including pain on injection, involuntary movements, transient apnea, and hypotension after induction of anesthesia. Numerous strategies have been proposed to reduce pain on injection, of which the use of a large antecubital vein or pretreatment with lidocaine in conjunction

<!-- PDF page 12 -->

with venous occlusion are the most effective,<sup>190</sup> and superior to altering the formulation of propofol.<sup>191</sup>

Some of the adverse effects of propofol can be minimized by reducing the propofol dose by co- induction with adjuvant drugs, the most common being midazolam. Pretreatment with midazolam 0.1 mg/kg allowed anesthesia to be induced with a decreased propofol dose and also attenuated the resultant hemodynamic changes.<sup>192</sup> A substantial decrease in the propofol dose can be achieved even when midazolam is administered up to 10 minutes before induction of anesthesia, a technique that also can make the induction experience for the patient more pleasant.<sup>193</sup> However, this approach may delay recovery. Midazolam 0.03 mg/kg halved the required propofol dose but significantly impaired psychomotor recovery, even though awakening times were not delayed.<sup>194</sup> Co- induction with short- acting opioids, such as alfentanil, can improve the quality of induction and ease of laryngeal mask airway (LMA) insertion, but at the expense of an increased incidence of hypotension and prolonged apnea.<sup>195</sup> Similarly, fentanyl reduced propofol dose and improved conditions for LMA insertion, but also prolonged respiratory depression.<sup>196</sup> Furthermore, the use of 1 mcg/kg<sup>197</sup> or fixed doses of 75 mcg<sup>198</sup> to 100 mcg<sup>199</sup> of perioperative fentanyl increase the incidence of PONV. Giving an initial bolus of 30 mg of propofol reduced the total propofol induction dose to a degree similar to that achieved by 2 mg of midazolam.<sup>200</sup> This technique, which has been termed propofol autocoinduction,<sup>201</sup> can reduce propofol dose and hypotension to a degree comparable to that with midazolam pretreatment but without delaying recovery. Following the concepts of opioid minimization and enhanced recovery after surgery, propofol co- induction can also be performed with intravenous lidocaine 1 mg/kg.

The pharmacokinetics of propofol allow it to be used as a variable-rate infusion to maintain anesthesia. Recovery after anesthesia maintained with propofol is typically no faster compared to volatile anesthetics after an induction dose of propofol,<sup>189</sup> and initial awakening may be delayed compared to that with the shorter- acting inhaled anethetics.<sup>202</sup> These differences in emergence times are no more than 2 to 3 minutes<sup>203</sup> and so not of clinical significance. Recovery to home readiness may occur up to 15 minutes sooner after propofol than isoflurane but is no faster than sevoflurane or desflurane. There are conflicting reports on whether propofol may be more<sup>204</sup> or less<sup>205</sup> likely than inhaled anesthetics to impair cognitive dysfunction after ambulatory anesthesia in older patients. One consistent feature of propofol anesthesia is a less frequent incidence of early PONV in contrast to the use of inhaled anesthetics.<sup>189,203,206</sup> However, this benefit is clinically significant only when the baseline incidence of PONV is very high.<sup>206</sup> The reduction in PONV achieved by total IV anesthesia (TIVA), specifically the use of propofol and the omission of N2O, was similar to that produced by the use of single- drug antiemetic prophylaxis.<sup>207</sup> Furthermore, any reduction in PONV from TIVA appears to be confined to the early recovery phase.<sup>208</sup>

Outside of the United States, delivery of propofol is increasingly performed by TCI, which makes the delivery of propofol easier, with fewer interventions required by the anesthesiologist, in contrast to manual infusions.<sup>209</sup> However, it does not improve the quality of anesthesia, shorten recovery time, or reduce adverse events,<sup>209,210</sup> based on limited literature

to date. A further issue is that TCI systems calculate the predicted plasma propofol concentration and considerable variation exists between this and the actual measured value.<sup>210</sup> Further sources of error are introduced when the effect site, rather than the plasma concentration, is targeted.<sup>211</sup> Currently two different pharmacokinetic models are in common use, both derived from healthy subjects. The choice of model may make relatively little difference in the young, fit patient, but performance differs considerably in older patients<sup>211</sup> and neither model is reliable in the morbidly obese.<sup>211,212</sup> Even newer pharmacokinetic models developed specifically with data from obese patients have a tendency to underestimate plasma propofol concentrations.<sup>213</sup>

### INHALED ANESTHETICS

Volatile anesthetics remain the most popular choice for the maintenance of ambulatory anesthesia because of their ease of administration, controllability, and rapid emergence. Halothane and enflurane are now of historical interest only, and use of isoflurane has declined considerably in ambulatory anesthesia as less- soluble, shorter- acting drugs have become available.

### Sevoflurane

The low solubility and minimal airway irritation of sevoflurane make it a readily controllable, short- acting anesthetic for ambulatory surgery.<sup>202</sup> In contrast to isoflurane, sevoflurane resulted in significantly earlier awakening and orientation, caused less postoperative drowsiness, and facilitated earlier discharge by an average of 25 minutes.<sup>203</sup> Compared to propofol, orientation occurred earlier but the time to home readiness was similar.<sup>203</sup> The lack of airway irritation with sevoflurane means that rapid increases in inspired concentration are well tolerated,<sup>214</sup> facilitating control of anesthetic depth. This is in contrast to the cough reflex<sup>215</sup> and transient increases in heart rate and blood pressure, which sudden changes in isoflurane or desflurane concentration may provoke.<sup>216</sup>

Lack of airway irritation makes sevoflurane ideal for inhaled induction, which may be especially desirable in children and needle- phobic adults.<sup>202</sup> An 8% bolus induction of sevoflurane in adult patients can be faster than with propofol, with a similar rate of different side effects.<sup>217</sup> Sevoflurane induction also caused significantly less depression of mean arterial pressure than propofol in older patients and was equally as well accepted as an induction choice.<sup>218</sup> Using sevoflurane for volatile induction and maintenance of anesthesia (VIMA) has several benefits for ambulatory patients, but is associated with a greater incidence of PONV than when propofol is used for induction, maintenance, or both, of anesthesia.<sup>202</sup> The increased PONV appears to be related to co- administered opioids, which are rarely needed with VIMA, and omitting the opioids minimizes the problem.<sup>197,217,219</sup> In children, the rapid emergence from sevoflurane (and desflurane) anesthesia is associated with a high incidence of emergence delirium, especially if inadequate measures are in place to control postoperative pain.<sup>220</sup> Numerous strategies have been evaluated to reduce emergence delirium. Midazolam premedication is ineffective, but supplemental use of fentanyl 1 mcg/kg, propofol, ketamine, and α2- adrenergic agonists all reduce

<!-- PDF page 13 -->

agitation to some degree.<sup>157</sup> Although emergence delirium is undesirable, it is not associated with any long- term consequences and does not delay recovery room discharge.<sup>221</sup> There is insufficient evidence of adequate quality to show whether propofol TIVA reduces the risk of behavioral disturbances (or PONV) compared with inhaled anesthesia.<sup>222</sup>

### Desflurane

With its low blood solubility, desflurane should be an ideal anesthetic for ambulatory anesthesia. However, metaanalysis of 25 randomized studies showed that patients receiving desflurane followed commands, had their trachea extubated, and were oriented only 1.0 to 1.2 minutes earlier than those receiving sevoflurane for up to 3.1 hours.<sup>223</sup> No differences were found in recovery room stay<sup>224</sup> or incidence of PONV.<sup>223</sup> Concerns about the more frequent incidence of airway irritation with desflurane may limit the rapidity with which anesthetic depth can be altered,<sup>217</sup> although relatively few problems were encountered in patients spontaneously breathing desflurane through an LMA when co- administered with fentanyl.<sup>225,226</sup> Although desflurane has lower fat solubility than sevoflurane, the rate of diffusion into the fat compartment is extremely slow with either inhaled anesthetic; therefore, lower fat solubility does not make the case for use of desflurane as the ideal agent for morbidly obese patients unless procedure length is very long. In practice, some studies of ambulatory surgery in the morbidly obese have found more rapid recovery after desflurane than sevoflurane,<sup>227</sup> whereas others found emergence and recovery to be similar with either drug.<sup>228</sup> As desflurane has a disproportionately high climate impact with a life- cycle greenhouse gas emission 15 times that of isoflurane and 20 times that of sevoflurane, consideration of the use of desflurane should weigh its negative environmental impact as a greenhouse gas in the context of patient benefit.<sup>229,230</sup>

### ANESTHETIC ADJUVANTS

Some adjuvant drugs are administered to supplement the effects of general anesthesia and in an effort to reduce adverse effects.

### Nitrous Oxide

Although N2O is by far the oldest anesthetic drug still in use, its role is regularly questioned. N2O is weakly emetogenic, yet its omission only significantly decreases vomiting when the baseline incidence is high and has no effect on nausea or the complete control of PONV.<sup>231</sup> Others have found the effects of omitting N2O to be “modest.”<sup>232</sup> This may be because the alternatives, specifically the use of supplemental opioids or higher inhaled anesthetic concentration, also induce PONV. The effect of N2O on PONV appears to be time dependent. There was no clinically significant effect from at least an hour’s exposure,<sup>233</sup> allowing N2O to retain a place in modern ambulatory anesthesia, where its use also improves the quality and speed of induction of anesthesia, facilitates faster recovery, and reduces overall costs.<sup>234</sup>

### Opioid Analgesics

The indiscriminate use of opioid analgesics should be discouraged in ambulatory surgery to avoid PONV and

unplanned admission.<sup>235,236</sup> Although long- lasting drugs such as morphine are especially detrimental, even the ultrashort- acting opioid remifentanil (discussed in more detail below) resulted in a 35% incidence of PONV when used with desflurane and multimodal prophylaxis, compared with only 4% when all opioids were avoided.<sup>237</sup> For minor to intermediate ambulatory surgery procedures, the routine administration of as little as 1 mcg/kg of fentanyl serves only to increase the incidence of PONV,<sup>238</sup> and its omission does not worsen postoperative pain, provided prophylactic analgesia is ensured with local anesthetic infiltration and preoperative NSAIDs.<sup>197</sup> However, small doses of fentanyl may be useful to provide additional postoperative analgesia when given near the end of more painful procedures.

Remifentanil is a μ- opioid receptor agonist with a rapid onset and short duration of action. These qualities make it an appealing drug for the ambulatory setting. However, concerns exist with the use of remifentanil due to the association with acute opioid tolerance and hyperalgesia, with some evidence that remifentanil may more readily cause tolerance and hyperalgesia than other opioids.<sup>239</sup>

Some remifentanil- related postoperative pain may be attributed to abrupt drug withdrawal. However, high- dose remifentanil may also cause aggravated postoperative pain independent of drug cessation, especially when administering high cumulative doses >40–80 mcg/kg for major surgery. Lower cumulative doses have not been consistently associated with acute tolerance or hyperalgesia in that setting.<sup>240</sup> A meta- analysis of 21 randomized controlled trials (RCTs) concluded that high intraoperative doses of remifentanil (i.e. 3 mcg/mg/min or greater) are associated with a small but statistically significant increase in acute pain, as measured by pain scores at rest and opioid consumption in the first 24 hours after surgery.<sup>241</sup> Looking specifically at ambulatory surgery, it is notable that intraoperative remifentanil use was associated with the highest need for postoperative fentanyl after laparoscopic cholecystectomy compared with intraoperative fentanyl or esmolol.<sup>242</sup> Particularly concerning is that opioid- induced hyperalgesia may last up to a week,<sup>243</sup> and a possible correlation between acute surgical wound hyperalgesia and chronic pain may exist.<sup>244</sup> When using remifentanil, and all opioid analgesics, multimodal analgesia with nonopioid medications and local/regional analgesia is an essential part of controlling postoperative pain.

### Cardiovascular Drugs Including Esmolol

Although intraoperative hemodynamic disturbances are usually managed by increasing the primary anesthetic concentration, administering an opioid analgesic, or both, it may be more appropriate to treat these responses with cardiovascular drugs. One study found that using an infusion of esmolol as an alternative to alfentanil to control heart rate reduced emergence times after arthroscopy.<sup>245</sup> Additionally, substituting esmolol for remifentanil reduced nausea after gynecologic laparoscopy,<sup>237</sup> and reduced early postoperative opioid consumption and postanesthesia care unit (PACU) length of stay after laparoscopic cholecystectomy.<sup>242</sup> Using a combination of esmolol and nicardipine to blunt increases in heart rate and arterial blood pressure, respectively, prevented the need for increases in inspired anesthetic concentration and consequently shortened

<!-- PDF page 14 -->

emergence and recovery times.<sup>246</sup> Ambulatory patients who received esmolol intraoperatively for hemodynamic control also had significantly smaller requirements for postoperative opioid analgesia.<sup>246,247</sup> Administration of a continuous infusion of esmolol during laparoscopic gynecologic surgery reduced sevoflurane consumption by 18%, shortened recovery room stay, reduced postoperative pain scores, and reduced fentanyl requirements.<sup>248</sup> In a similar study, an esmolol infusion decreased intraoperative remifentanil requirements and again resulted in lower postoperative pain scores and halved the dose of rescue fentanyl.<sup>249</sup> Labetalol may be a cost- effective alternative to esmolol for longer cases, especially in older patients in whom it is less likely to cause unintended hypotension.<sup>250</sup>

### Neuromuscular Blocking Drugs

Neuromuscular blocking drugs (NMBDs) may be used in ambulatory anesthesia to facilitate endotracheal intubation or to provide profound surgical relaxation. Although several of the available compounds are suitable, muscle pain after administration of succinylcholine and concerns about residual neuromuscular block from intermediate- duration NMBDs after brief procedures have fueled the search for alternatives. The search for a nondepolarizing equivalent to succinylcholine has been unsuccessful to date. The most promising candidate, rapacuronium, was withdrawn from clinical use because of a frequent incidence of severe bronchospasm, although other factors such as inconvenient administration, declining use of endotracheal intubation, and high costs also contributed to its lack of commercial success.<sup>251</sup> The search for a rapid- onset, short- acting nondepolarizing NMBD continues with investigation of several fumarate compounds, of which gantacurium was found to have rapid onset and brief duration, but was also associated with histamine release.<sup>252</sup> Subsequent attention has moved toward designing a molecule of intermediate duration, but which can be rapidly reversed at any time by administering L- cysteine.<sup>252</sup>

An alternative to short- acting NMBDs is terminating the neuromuscular blockade with sugammadex, which can rapidly and completely reverse rocuronium (or vecuronium) independent of the degree of residual neuromuscular block.<sup>253</sup> Sugammadex is very expensive compared to neostigmine, although in the United States the cost of neostigmine has risen dramatically. Currently no reliable evaluations have been done on the cost- effectiveness of sugammadex in routine clinical practice. Although improved recovery times resulting from the elimination of residual neuromuscular block by sugammadex may potentially be cost- effective, this depends on the productivity of staff during the time saved and would probably require major changes to the workflow for actual benefits to be realized.<sup>253</sup>

Endotracheal intubation also can be achieved without the use of NMBDs, thereby avoiding all of their adverse effects. This practice is most common in children, for procedures in which airway protection, but not prolonged muscle relaxation, is required. The optimum technique depends on personal experience and preference, but most commonly involves deep sevoflurane anesthesia<sup>254</sup> or propofol supplemented by remifentanil or alfentanil.<sup>255</sup> For adults, a dose of remifentanil 3 mcg/kg with propofol 2 mg/kg

is recommended,<sup>256</sup> but satisfactory conditions may be obtained with remifentanil 2 mcg/kg, thereby reducing the side effects of bradycardia and hypotension.

### AIRWAY MANAGEMENT

Many ambulatory surgical patients can be managed with a supraglottic airway (SGA), which results in a significantly less frequent incidence of sore throat, hoarseness, coughing, and laryngospasm compared to inserting a tracheal tube.<sup>257</sup> The SGA can occasionally cause pressure trauma to a variety of cranial nerves, in particular the recurrent laryngeal nerve, whereas hoarseness and vocal cord injuries are more common after the use of endotracheal intubation during short- term anesthesia.<sup>258</sup> SGAs are relatively easy to insert with patients in the prone position,<sup>259</sup> making it a simple way of managing procedures such as pilonidal sinus repair or surgery to the short saphenous vein.

Traditionally, endotracheal intubation has been advocated for laparoscopic procedures and surgery in obese patients, but further development of first- generation SGAs, such as the laryngeal mask airway (LMA) into secondgeneration SGAs, combined with more confidence in their use may be changing this. Compared to the original LMA, second- generation SGAs provide increased seal pressure, reduce gastric inflation, provide for gastric drainage, and therefore allow potentially better protection against aspiration of gastric contents while maintaining similar insertion characteristics.<sup>260</sup> At least one large consecutive series by an experienced user appears to support these benefits in routine clinical practice, although without the structure of a clinical trial.<sup>261</sup> During laparoscopic cholecystectomy, a second- generation SGA, the ProSeal LMA, provided adequate pulmonary ventilation without gastric distention in nonobese patients, although it was somewhat less effective than endotracheal intubation in the obese.<sup>262</sup> A review article concluded that second- generation supraglottic airways achieve adequate ventilation during laparoscopic cholecystectomy with a very low incidence of regurgitation and aspiration, but cannot yet be regarded as completely safe.<sup>263</sup>

Provided safety can be ensured, avoiding endotracheal intubation by using a second generation SGA for laparoscopic surgery appears to offer considerable advantages, including smoother emergence with significantly less coughing.<sup>262,264</sup> In laparoscopic gynecologic procedures, the ProSeal LMA was associated with lower pain scores at 2 and 6 hours, with reduced analgesic requirements and less nausea at the same times.<sup>265</sup> Similarly, in females undergoing gynecologic laparoscopic or breast surgery, it reduced the absolute risk for PONV by 40% and also reduced sore throat, analgesic requirements, and recovery room stay.<sup>266</sup> During laparoscopic gastric banding, the ProSeal LMA reduced the intraoperative stress response and also shortened recovery room stay and hospital discharge.<sup>267</sup>

Since the expiration of LMA patents, many manufacturers have introduced similar designs, often using different materials to produce a low- cost disposable product. Often little or no data are available on the effectiveness or safety of these generic products.<sup>268</sup> Furthermore, the success of the LMA led to the development of many new supraglottic airways of myriad designs. Some of these may offer advantages

<!-- PDF page 15 -->

in ambulatory surgery,<sup>269</sup> although there is often little or no comparative data with the LMA. Anesthesiologists should therefore be wary of the claims made for new airway devices until they have been adequately evaluated.<sup>270</sup>

One supraglottic airway that has been extensively evaluated is the i- gel, which has a contoured non- inflating cuff and gastric drainage channel. The i- gel is faster to insert and results in less sore throat than the LMA with a seal pressure intermediate between first- and second- generation SGAs.<sup>271</sup>

### REGIONAL ANESTHESIA

Regional anesthesia (see also Chapters 41 and 42) may make ambulatory surgery accessible to some patients for whom the risks of general anesthesia are excessive. It also increases patient choice, allows participation in intraoperative decision- making (e.g., in sports injuries), expedites recovery, and can provide beneficial postoperative analgesia.

### Spinal Anesthesia

Various ambulatory surgical procedures, such as transurethral prostatectomy, female incontinence surgery, ankle and foot surgery, and lower extremity arthroplasty, are well suited to spinal anesthesia (see also Chapter 41). As finegauge, pencil- point spinal needles have reduced the incidence of significant post–dural puncture headache to 0.5% to 1%,<sup>272</sup> the main challenge of spinal anesthesia in ambulatory surgery is to prevent prolonged motor block delaying discharge or physical therapy assessment. Although lidocaine has an optimal duration for ambulatory spinal anesthesia, its incidence of transient neurologic syndrome (TNS), estimated as high as 18%, has resulted in relative cessation of use of lidocaine.<sup>273</sup> TNS is not limited to lidocaine and has been reported with prilocaine,<sup>274</sup> 2- chloroprocaine,<sup>273,275</sup> mepivacaine,<sup>273</sup> and bupivacaine.<sup>276</sup> However, a 2019 Cochrane systematic review noted no increased risk with prilocaine, procaine, ropivacaine, bupivacaine, or levobupivacaine, but an increased risk of TNS with spinal chloroprocaine and mepivacaine.<sup>273</sup> Notably, TNS was only noted in one small study for chloroprocaine, and mepivacaine studies had issues with inadequate blinding and loss of patients to follow- up.

Optimal spinal anesthesia provides rapid onset, predictable resolution, and timely return of motor function and spontaneous voiding. Lower dose bupivacaine (≤10 mg) is associated with decreased time to urination and discharge home; however, recovery time is less predictable and can be prolonged.<sup>277</sup> While unilateral spinal anesthesia may be achieved with low- dose bupivacaine (5–7.5 mg) by placing the patient operative side dependent for 10–15 minutes, block failure rates are increased with these low doses, dense motor block should not be expected, and recovery times may persist for 3 to 6 hours. Additionally, spinal anesthesia may make postoperative urinary retention more likely in older patients when >7 mg of bupivacaine is used,<sup>278</sup> and this risk is particularly high after inguinal hernia surgery<sup>279</sup> where local anesthetic infiltration may offer sufficient anesthesia.<sup>176,280</sup>

As spinal bupivacaine may cause unacceptable discharge delays, prilocaine, chloroprocaine, and mepivacaine are

receiving new attention for ambulatory surgery.<sup>179,180,181</sup> Hyperbaric prilocaine has a faster onset and shorter duration than the plain preparation,<sup>281</sup> with patients receiving 40 mg of 2% hyperbaric prilocaine being home ready in 208 ± 68 minutes.<sup>282</sup> This is comparable to discharge times reported with low- dose bupivacaine plus fentanyl,<sup>283</sup> although 13% of the patients receiving hyperbaric prilocaine required supplementation for inadequate analgesia toward the end of surgery, which was scheduled to last for less than an hour.<sup>282</sup> Doses of 40 to 60 mg achieve analgesia of about 90 minutes duration for lower abdominal or limb procedures, while 10 to 30 mg is adequate for perineal surgery of up to 40 minutes, with home discharge usually achievable within 4 hours.<sup>281</sup> Spinal 2- chloroprocaine has an even shorter and faster recovery than low- dose bupivacaine<sup>283</sup> and is comparable<sup>284</sup> or faster<sup>181</sup> than lidocaine. For limb surgery, 40- and 50- mg doses had longer block regression (2.13 ± 0.46 and 2.23 ± 0.38 hours, respectively) than 30 mg (1.76 ± 0.35 hours); however, the 30- mg dose had an increased failure rate.<sup>285</sup> The addition of fentanyl appears to extend the block duration of both 2- chloroprocaine<sup>275</sup> and prilocaine<sup>281</sup> with little delay in recovery in either case. However, as use of intrathecal opioids may also increase postoperative nausea and vomiting, pruritus and urinary retention,<sup>277</sup> routine use of intrathecal additives is not recommended for the ambulatory setting.<sup>179,286</sup>

With increased focus on same- day discharge after lower extremity arthroplasty, spinal mepivacaine has also received renewed interest. Patients receiving mepivacaine (52.5-60 mg) for total hip arthroplasty were more likely to ambulate 3–3.5 hours after spinal placement and achieve same- day discharge compared to patients receiving bupivacaine (11.25 mg hyperbaric or 12.5 mg isobaric), although patients in the mepivacaine group did have increased pain in the early postoperative period.<sup>276</sup> Notably, TNS symptoms were noted in 10% of mepivacaine patients, but did not differ from hyperbaric (11.3%) or isobaric (3.9%) bupivacaine subjects (Table 68.3).

### Epidural Anesthesia

Epidural analgesia is seldom used in adult ambulatory anesthesia. Although it allows the block duration to be extended by using a catheter technique, this is offset by the time required in establishing the block and less certainty of success, as well as the risk for inadvertent intravascular injection or dural puncture. In knee arthroscopy, recovery after 15 to 20 mL of epidural 3% 2- chloroprocaine was more rapid than spinal procaine with 20 mcg fentanyl<sup>287</sup> and also had a decreased incidence of pruritus. Recovery after epidural 3% 2- chloroprocaine required fewer top- up injections and permitted discharge an hour sooner after ambulatory knee arthroscopy than with 1.5% epidural lidocaine.<sup>288</sup> However, the use of epidural 2- chloroprocaine has a more frequent incidence of back pain.<sup>289</sup>

In children, caudal epidural analgesia – for example, using levobupivacaine 0.5 to 1 mL/kg of 0.25% – is popular for postoperative pain relief.<sup>69</sup> The technique is most useful for bilateral surgery or other situations in which the maximum safe dose would not permit an adequate volume for wound infiltration. After circumcision, caudal analgesia was no better than parenteral or systemic analgesia or dorsal nerve block in reducing analgesic requirements, nausea,

<!-- PDF page 16 -->

or vomiting.<sup>290</sup> However, motor block and leg weakness were significantly more common in patients receiving a caudal block. The addition of clonidine<sup>291</sup> or dexmedetomidine<sup>292</sup> augments caudal analgesia, but concerns remain about the frequency of sedative and hemodynamic side effects and the risk for neurotoxicity.<sup>156,290</sup>

### Intravenous Regional Anesthesia

Intravenous regional anesthesia (IVRA) (Bier block) is a simple, reliable method of anesthesia for upper limb surgery and is sometimes also used in the lower extremity.<sup>293</sup> In the United Kingdom and Europe, prilocaine is the preferred local anesthetic because of its high therapeutic index.<sup>294</sup> Lidocaine has also been used for many years,<sup>293</sup> and appears to be a safe alternative.<sup>295</sup> Ropivacaine has also been extensively evaluated for IVRA. Compared to lidocaine, 0.2% to 0.375% ropivacaine resulted in prolonged and improved postoperative analgesia.<sup>296–298</sup> However, compared to prilocaine, ropivacaine’s onset was slower, no useful prolongation of postoperative analgesia occurred, and ropivacaine plasma concentrations were more than double those of prilocaine, despite a 60% lower dose.<sup>294</sup>

IVRA may offer a cost- effective alternative to general anesthesia for outpatient hand surgery, as it is easy to administer with faster recovery and fewer postoperative complications.<sup>299</sup> However, analgesia may be inadequate in some cases, and require supplemental local

anesthesia, repeat of the block, or conversion to general anesthesia.<sup>299,300</sup> Compared to mepivacaine (280 mg) axillary nerve block for hand surgery, IVRA with lidocaine (300 mg) was found noninferior. Notably, induction times were longer for the axillary block group, while tourniquet discomfort, intraoperative pain scores, and postoperative pain scores were higher in the IVRA group.<sup>301</sup>

A variety of IVRA adjuvants have been described to decrease tourniquet pain, improve block quality, and prolong analgesia after cuff deflation.<sup>301</sup> Opioids are relatively ineffective and cause nausea, vomiting, and dizziness after tourniquet deflation,<sup>301</sup> but several NSAIDs have been shown to be beneficial. Lornoxicam<sup>302</sup> decreased tourniquet pain and improved postoperative analgesia and tenoxicam improved postoperative pain,<sup>301</sup> but the effects of ketorolac are unclear after most of the evidence showing its efficacy in IVRA was withdrawn.<sup>162</sup> Dexamethasone improved both block quality and postoperative analgesia.<sup>303</sup> Similar results have been observed with the α2- adrenoceptor agonist dexmedetomidine,<sup>304,305</sup> whereas clonidine delayed tourniquet pain without improving postoperative analgesia.<sup>306</sup>

### Peripheral Nerve Blocks and Infiltration

A wide range of regional anesthetic techniques can be used to facilitate ambulatory surgery or provide postoperative analgesia (Table 68.4).<sup>307</sup> While peripheral nerve block techniques and indications are reviewed in greater detail

**Table 68.3** Local Anesthetics for Spinal Anesthesia

|Bupivacaine (0.5%, 0.75%)|5–15 mg|90–240 mins|240–480 mins|Prolonged blockade|

_TNS_ , transient neurologic syndrome.

**Table 68.4** Core Peripheral Nerve Blocks for Ambulatory Surgery

*Adductor canal block should be performed in addition to the popliteal block if medial ankle coverage is needed. Doses are approximations. The lowest effective dose should be utilized and within a safe dosing range considering a patient’s weight, age, and medical comorbidities.

![第 68 章，原始 PDF 第 16 頁](assets/chapter-68/source-page-0016.png)

:::

<!-- PDF page 17 -->

in Chapter 42, the ambulatory anesthesiologist may not necessarily need to know a vast array of regional techniques but should be proficient with a core set of peripheral blocks.<sup>308,309</sup> The applicability of these techniques depends on the nature of the planned procedure, the patient, the preferences of the surgeon and anesthesiologist, and the skill and experience of the anesthesiologist performing the block.

Benefits of peripheral nerve blocks include excellent postoperative analgesia and reduced PONV, but this must be balanced with patient discomfort while performing the block, the transition to satisfactory analgesia with block cessation, and the risk for perioperative nerve injuries. Despite the benefits of regional anesthesia, a retrospective study of almost 13 million patients in the US found peripheral nerve blocks were placed in only 3.3% of appropriate ambulatory surgery cases.<sup>310</sup> While regional anesthesia can have a higher failure rate in inexperienced hands, especially in morbidly obese patients, ultrasound guidance has reduced times for peripheral nerve block placement and onset while increasing block analgesia duration and success.<sup>311</sup> However, there is no conclusive evidence that ultrasound guidance reduces the incidence of peripheral nerve injury.<sup>312</sup>

Simpler locoregional techniques may be more appropriate for some procedures. Intra- articular local anesthesia produces a moderate and relatively brief reduction in postoperative pain after arthroscopic knee surgery, but this is still considered to be of clinical significance in ambulatory surgery.<sup>313</sup> In many cases, simple wound infiltration may be as effective as central and proximal peripheral blocks and allows patients to mobilize more quickly.<sup>314</sup> While previous reports asserted that the risk of systemic toxicity resulting from large volumes of local anesthetic infiltration were unfounded,<sup>314</sup> more recent reviews reported that 11% of reported local anesthetic systemic toxicity (LAST) cases occurred following subcutaneous infiltration, 14% from intraurethral or intravaginal administration, 17% from airway infiltration or topicalization, and others from stellate ganglion blocks and liposuction.<sup>315</sup> Furthermore, in a survey of providers outside the field of anesthesia, only 2% were aware that lipid emulsion should be used in the treatment of LAST.<sup>316</sup>

Infiltration anesthesia offers advantages for many ambulatory procedures. Utilization of local infiltration versus general anesthesia was associated with decreased operative times, recovery room stay, and postoperative complications compared to general anesthesia, regardless of age.<sup>280,317</sup> Infiltration anesthesia also avoids the increased risk for urinary retention associated with spinal anesthesia, especially in older patients.<sup>280</sup> Simple wound infiltration has been modified to local infiltration analgesia (LIA) for major orthopedic ambulatory surgery. Originally developed for analgesia after major knee and hip surgery,<sup>318</sup> the original technique involved infiltration of a mixture (300 mg ropivacaine, 30 mg ketorolac, 1.5 mg epinephrine diluted to 150–200 mL with saline) into all the tissue planes of the surgical field over approximately an hour and a catheter was placed into the wound to permit later repeat dosing. In the original series of 325 patients, mainly undergoing elective hip resurfacing but including some primary hip and knee replacements, pain was well controlled (numerical

pain score 0–3) without morphine in two- thirds of patients, most of whom could walk with assistance 5 to 6 hours after surgery; 71% were discharged, walking independently after a single- night stay (Fig. 68.2).<sup>318</sup> In a blinded, randomized trial in patients undergoing unicompartmental knee arthroplasty, LIA significantly reduced pain and opioid consumption and decreased the median length of stay by 2 days compared with placebo, and 68% of patients were discharged after a 1- night stay.<sup>319</sup> Compared with no injection or placebo, systematic reviews conclude that LIA is effective for acute pain management after knee arthroplasty<sup>320,321</sup> in the first postoperative 24 hours; however, LIA adds little additional analgesia after hip surgery when a multimodal regimen is also used.<sup>320,322,323</sup>

A limitation of infiltration anesthesia is the relatively short duration of pain relief it provides. Continuous wound or perineural catheter techniques can extend analgesia duration, reducing pain scores, opioid- related side effects, and length of hospital stay, as well as improving patient satisfaction.<sup>324</sup> Since catheters may be more complex to place and require organized home follow- up, avenues to prolong single- injection techniques are constantly sought. Local anesthetic adjuncts are easily added, but the quality of evidence remains low, with high heterogeneity among studies as additives are frequently not approved for perineural use.<sup>325,326</sup> Although not approved by the US Food and Drug Administration (FDA) for perineural use, perineural and intravenous dexamethasone have shown promising results in prolonging both perineural analgesia and reducing rebound pain,<sup>327</sup> which can occur in up to 50% of patients after ambulatory orthopedic surgery.<sup>328</sup>

**Novel Local Anesthetics.** Novel local anesthetics have also been developed to extend analgesic duration. Encapsulation of bupivacaine into a biodegradable carrier offers the prospect of potential analgesic effects for 96 hours.<sup>329</sup> Widespread application requires that the large doses of local anesthetic contained within the carrier are not rapidly released, leading to toxicity, and that the encapsulating materials do not break down into harmful products.<sup>330</sup> One extended- duration preparation of bupivacaine, Exparel, which uses an established liposomal- based drug delivery system (Lipo Foam, Contour<sup>MD</sup> ), is currently approved by the FDA. Although early industry- sponsored trials were promising compared to placebo, systematic reviews and meta- analyses have not shown Exparel to provide superior analgesia compared to non- liposomal bupivacaine when used for surgical site or periarticular infiltration<sup>331,332</sup> or peripheral nerve block analgesia.<sup>331,332</sup> The acidity of the surgical wound is theorized as a reason liposomal bupivacaine may not have performed as anticipated.

Another extended- release local anesthetic, HTX- 011 (Zynrelef), combines bupivacaine with low- dose meloxicam to normalize the local pH in the incision.<sup>333</sup> The bupivacaine–meloxicam combination is formulated in a polymer and applied in the desired area with a needleless syringe prior to wound closure. This drug received European Commission authorization in September of 2020 and approval by the FDA in May 2021. Phase 2b and 3 studies examined HTX011 (400 mg bupivacaine/12 mg meloxicam) versus both bupivacaine alone and placebo, and found that bupivacaine/

meloxicam reduced postoperative pain and opioid consumption through 72 hours following primary total knee arthroplasty,<sup>334</sup> bunionectomy,<sup>335</sup> and herniorrhaphy.<sup>336</sup> Compared to bupivacaine or placebo, subjects who received bupivacaine/meloxicam also had earlier discharge readiness after knee arthroplasty<sup>334</sup> and were more likely to remain opioid- free through 72 hours following bunionectomy<sup>335</sup> and herniorrhaphy.<sup>336</sup> Bupivacaine/meloxicam also appears to be effective and well tolerated in geriatric patients.<sup>337</sup>

Several naturally occurring alkaloid toxins, which may have the potential to provide longer-lasting and

safer local anesthesia, have been considered.<sup>330</sup> A preliminary study with neosaxitoxin, an extracellular sodium channel blocker, described superior analgesia compared to that with bupivacaine at 12 hours after laparoscopic cholecystectomy.<sup>338</sup> Subcutaneous injection of neosaxitoxin in combination with bupivacaine and epinephrine produced a five- fold increase in analgesic duration compared with bupivacaine alone, without an apparent increase in toxicity.<sup>339</sup> Tetrodotoxin, a reversible site 1 sodium channel toxin derived from shellfish and puffer fish, is also under investigation in animal models both

<!-- PDF page 19 -->

in and out of liposomes and in combinations with other medications.<sup>340,341</sup>

### SEDATION

Although some procedures may be performed with local or regional anesthesia alone, additional drugs are often required for anxiolysis, to provide additional analgesia and to attain suitable operating conditions by helping the patient lie still in an appropriate position. The level of sedation required varies with each therapeutic, diagnostic, or surgical procedure and must be individually adjusted to achieve a balance of patient comfort and safety.<sup>342</sup>

The ASA defines three levels of sedation according to the responsiveness of the patient.<sup>343</sup> With minimal sedation a degree of anxiolysis is reached, but the patient is normally responsive and has a clear airway. During moderate (conscious) sedation the patient is sleepier but responds purposefully to verbal or tactile stimuli. Spontaneous ventilation is usually adequate and interventions are not required to maintain a patent airway. With deep sedation, the patient can respond purposefully only to repeated or painful stimuli and may require a degree of airway or ventilatory support. Far from being discrete, these sedative levels lie on a continuum (Fig. 68.3), which becomes general anesthesia when the patient loses consciousness and is unarousable even with painful stimuli.<sup>343</sup> Careful monitoring is essential, and the standard of care for patients receiving sedation should be the same as for patients undergoing general or regional anesthesia and includes all of the usual aspects of anesthesia care.<sup>344</sup> Unfortunately, a widely held belief among physicians and patients is that sedation is a safer form of care,<sup>345</sup> but an ASA closed claims database analysis revealed that the risks for death or permanent brain damage are similar to those of general anesthesia.<sup>346</sup> Predictably, the greatest risk for harm comes from respiratory depression by opioids and sedative–hypnotic drugs, and many cases could have been prevented by better monitoring and improved vigilance.<sup>347</sup>

The US term _monitored anesthesia care_ (MAC) is sometimes erroneously used to describe sedation administered by an anesthesiologist. However, the ASA has a specific definition of MAC, which is a billing term used to describe an anesthesia service that includes all perianesthesia aspects of care.<sup>344</sup>


![](assets/chapter-68/68._Ambulatory_-Outpatient-_Anesthesia.pdf-0019-07.png)
