**Fig. 68.3 The continuum of sedation and general anesthesia.** (Reproduced from Ahuja M, Armstrong I. Sedation. In: Smith I, McWhinnie D, Jackson I, eds. _Day Case Surgery_ . London: Oxford University Press; 2012:109–132. With permission.)

MAC may include varying levels of sedation, including conversion to general anesthesia, when necessary. However, “if the patient loses consciousness and the ability to respond purposefully, the anesthesia care is a general anesthetic, irrespective of whether airway instrumentation is required.”<sup>344</sup> The State Operating Manual Guidance for the CMS Conditions of Participation for Anesthesia Services (42 CFR §482.52, Interpretive Guidelines) describe MAC similarly, as “anesthesia care that includes the monitoring of the patient by a practitioner who is qualified to administer anesthesia,” and includes deep sedation/analgesia, requiring delivery or supervision by an appropriate anesthesia professional.

### Choice of Sedatives

The specific requirements for supplemental sedative and analgesic drugs depend on the procedure undertaken, the skill of the operator in using local anesthesia, and the experiences and expectations of the individual patient. The use of pharmacologic agents can be reduced by compassionate patient management at every stage, preoperative explanation, and the use of distractions, such as deep breathing, conversation, and listening to music.<sup>345</sup> Adjuvant drugs should be chosen to deliver those specific aspects that are required,<sup>348</sup> such as reduction of anxiety, sedation to relieve boredom or aid immobility, and analgesia for pain that is not amenable to further infiltration of local anesthetic.

### Midazolam

Midazolam produces anxiolysis and dose- related hypnosis and is a popular component of sedation. It also produces marked antegrade amnesia. At times this may be beneficial, but it is not always welcomed by patients.<sup>349</sup> Midazolam is preferred to other benzodiazepines because it is watersoluble, does not cause venoirritation or pain on injection, is rapid- acting, and has a relatively short elimination halflife of about 2 to 4 hours. As sole anesthetic, a single dose of 0.05 to 0.1 mg/kg allows reasonably predictable recovery after brief procedures, but individual responses are highly variable.<sup>350</sup> Recovery is also considerably slower if repeat or prolonged administration is required.

### Propofol

Propofol is an excellent sedative–hypnotic because its pharmacokinetic properties result in a rapid recovery from the effects of a single bolus dose and a continuous infusion. Propofol is a relatively pure hypnotic, providing no analgesia and only moderate amnesia. Infusion rates of 25 to 75 mcg/ kg/min are typically used,<sup>351</sup> but the short duration of action facilitates titration to effect.

Because of the excellent conditions that can be achieved and the superiority of recovery compared with that of midazolam,<sup>352</sup> demand for propofol sedation is growing rapidly in areas such as endoscopy. Propofol can rapidly cause apnea, before loss of consciousness, even in sedative doses, and unintended transition to general anesthesia is a constant risk. The safety of non- anesthesiologist administration of propofol is therefore controversial. In the United Kingdom, the Royal College of Anaesthetists and the British Society of Gastroenterology jointly state that propofol sedation for complex upper GI endoscopic procedures should be administered only by an “appropriately trained anaesthetist.”<sup>353</sup> In the United States, the FDA package

<!-- PDF page 20 -->

insert states that propofol may be given “only by persons trained in the administration of general anesthesia,” which the ASA supports.<sup>354</sup> However, the American Society for Gastrointestinal Endoscopy considers propofol use safe by anyone “proficient in the management of upper and lower airway complications, including manual techniques for reestablishing airway patency” and holding at least basic life support certification.<sup>355</sup>

Advocates of endoscopist- administered propofol claim a very low rate of airway complications (0.1%) and a mortality (4 in 646,080) less than that associated with endoscopist- administered midazolam–opioid sedation.<sup>356</sup> Their data also reveal a wide range for the frequency with which rescue airway support was required, suggesting considerable variation, either in technique or willingness to intervene to protect the airway.<sup>356</sup> When anesthesiologists administered propofol for patients undergoing endoscopic retrograde cholangiopancreatography (ERCP), deeper levels of sedation or general anesthesia were often intended and sedation- related events were far more common (21%), especially in patients of higher ASA physical status classification, but were managed without adverse consequence or delay, and high levels of patient and operator satisfaction were achieved.<sup>357</sup> A more recent study of anesthesiologist- administered propofol sedation has revealed a very similar (23%) incidence of significant unplanned events.<sup>358</sup>

### Remimazolam

Remimazolam is an ester- based benzodiazepine designed to be rapidly broken down by carboxylesterase- 1 (CES- 1) to the inactive metabolite CNS7054 with negligible hypnotic activity. It produces hypnotic effects by binding selectively to γ-aminobutyric acid A (GABAA) receptors.<sup>359</sup> Remimazolam has a fast onset and a short clinical effect with only minimal accumulation. Remimazolam is approved in the US for the induction and maintenance of procedural sedation in adults, lasting 30 minutes or less. In the European Union (EU), it is approved with no specified duration.<sup>359</sup> Remimazolam may be reversed with flumazenil, but it is rarely needed due to remimazolam’s short duration of action. The concurrent use of fentanyl with remimazolam may increase the incidence of adverse drug reactions.<sup>359</sup> Remimazolam has a similar effect on heart rate and blood pressure compared with midazolam<sup>360–362</sup> and less hypotension and need for vasopressors compared with propofol.<sup>363,364</sup> In clinical trials, remimazolam had similar or fewer events of desaturation or respiratory depression than midazolam.<sup>364</sup> Remimazolam was successfully tested in procedural anesthesia such as colonoscopy, upper gastrointestinal endoscopy, and bronchoscopy<sup>360–362</sup> with a procedure success rate >80%<sup>359</sup> and was used safely in high- risk patients (ASA 3 and 4) undergoing colonoscopy.<sup>361</sup>

The dosage of remimazolam should be titrated according to the desired sedation level and procedure and taking into account the patient’s general condition. The recommended dose is 2.5–5 mg as an initial bolus and re- dosing of 1.25–2.5 mg as needed. In the EU, the initial dose may be increased to 7 mg if no supplemental opioids are used.

The sedative effects of remimazolam may be increased in severe hepatic impairment<sup>359</sup> ; however, a clinical trial showed no requirement for dose adjustments in subjects

with hepatic or renal impairment.<sup>365</sup> Remimazolam has low oral bioavailability.<sup>359</sup> Attempts to use remimazolam intranasally show promising results.<sup>366</sup>

### Analgesic Adjuvants for Sedation

Opioids are useful for procedures in which pain cannot reliably be blocked with local anesthesia alone.<sup>342</sup> Compared to alfentanil in women undergoing breast biopsy, remifentanil resulted in lower pain scores during deep dissection and reduced the need for supplemental local anesthesia.<sup>367</sup> Remifentanil was a useful adjuvant to propofol sedation during transvaginal tape insertion<sup>368</sup> and in achieving optimal conditions for awake craniotomy,<sup>369</sup> surgery that is increasingly being performed as a day and short- stay procedure.<sup>28</sup> Remifentanil produced lower pain scores than fentanyl in patients receiving propofol sedation during hysteroscopy, but it did not improve recovery or patient satisfaction.<sup>370</sup> For hysteroscopic surgery, paracervical block supplemented by a remifentanil infusion permitted earlier mobilization and discharge compared to TIVA, and the sedation technique was also preferred by a higher proportion of patients. Remifentanil is usually delivered by infusion, but intermittent bolus doses may be more effective for some procedures.<sup>371</sup> Hyperalgesia after remifentanil administration remains a concern, as described above.

Both clonidine and dexmedetomidine have potentially useful analgesic, anxiolytic, and sedative properties. The slow onset and offset of clonidine and frequent reports of cardiovascular instability mean that neither drug has established a routine place in sedation,<sup>156,372</sup> although dexmedetomidine may be a useful analgesic adjuvant in awake craniotomy.<sup>373</sup> Ketamine improves analgesia when used to supplement propofol sedation, but an increase in PONV, psychomimetic side effects, and delay in discharge may be observed at higher doses.<sup>374</sup>

### Delivery of Sedation

Because of the varying needs to provide combinations of hypnosis, anxiolysis, analgesia, and amnesia, use of drug combinations is common during anesthesiologistadministered sedation. Preceding a propofol infusion with a 2- mg dose of midazolam improved anxiety, sedation, and amnesia for early intraoperative events with no detrimental effect on postoperative sedation, amnesia, or recovery times.<sup>375</sup> However, using drug combinations increases the risk for interactions, leading to adverse effects. At sedative doses, propofol and remifentanil each have only a modest effect on heart rate and arterial blood pressure, but their effect on respiration is strikingly synergistic, with the potential for severe respiratory depression.<sup>376</sup> Respiratory depression is also enhanced by the interaction of midazolam and remifentanil.<sup>377</sup> Antagonists of benzodiazepines and opioids may be useful in cases of unintended overdose, but should not be routinely relied on to reverse deep sedation because of their short duration of action, which may permit resedation before<sup>378</sup> or after discharge.<sup>379</sup> It is also possible to achieve high rates of patient satisfaction without procedural recall when using intermittent bolus doses of propofol alone for endoscopic procedures.<sup>380</sup>

As with TIVA, the stability and control of sedation regimens can be improved by using TCI systems.<sup>381</sup> Target concentrations of 0.5 to 2 mcg/mL for propofol and 0.5 to 1 ng/mL for

<!-- PDF page 21 -->

remifentanil are typical,<sup>342,372</sup> but should be titrated to individual effect. Effective sedation is usually judged by clinical end points or scores. Bispectral index (BIS) (see below section on Monitoring Depth of Anesthesia) changes with increasing sedation, but is too variable<sup>382</sup> to be useful routinely and was not effective as an end point for midazolam sedation.<sup>383</sup> BIS monitoring did not improve the quality of sedation or reduce the propofol dose or rates of hypoxemic, bradycardic, or hypotensive complications during ERCP.<sup>384</sup> However, BIS- guided TCI propofol may be useful in the management of patients with intellectual disability,<sup>385</sup> in whom clinical signs may be less reliable. Allowing patients to adjust their own sedation level by patient- controlled sedation resulted in less propofol being used compared with a continuous infusion and was significantly more popular with patients.<sup>386</sup> However, this approach necessarily requires comparatively light levels of sedation and appears to be an undesirable option for a significant proportion of patients.<sup>387</sup>

Inhaled sedation by a low dose of sevoflurane is an alternative technique that provides good control of sedation and rapid recovery.<sup>388,389</sup> However, it can be complicated by a frequent incidence of perioperative excitement and an increased risk for transition to general anesthesia.<sup>390</sup>

## Monitoring Depth of Anesthesia

Technology to monitor the impact of anesthetic medications on the central nervous system has lagged behind other physiologic monitors and is not considered a standard of care. However, processed electroencephalography (EEG) and cerebral oximetry and are increasingly discussed for perioperative brain health and may be beneficial in specific ambulatory populations.

### PROCESSED ELECTROENCEPHALOGRAPHY (EEG) AND CEREBRAL OXIMETRY

As EEG signals can be used as a surrogate measure of hypnosis, a variety of EEG- derived indices have been developed to measure the hypnotic component of anesthesia. While the number of products has grown and they use comparable principles, they differ in their utilized algorithms, which have proprietary information.<sup>391</sup> With a primary focus on the degree of hypnosis, EEG- derived scaled indices commonly range from 100 (“awake”) to 0 (“no detectable brain electrical activity”) and are inversely correlated with the hypnotic drug concentrations.<sup>392</sup> BIS was the first licensed monitor and is the most extensively studied. Other processed EEG devices available worldwide include SedLine, Entropy, and Narcotrend.<sup>393</sup>

More recently and less-studied, monitors have also investigated measuring sympathetic system responsiveness (“analgesic”) indices.<sup>394–397</sup> Notably, EEG- derived indices have overall not demonstrated an impact on patient outcomes.<sup>398</sup>

In ambulatory anesthesia, interest has focused on whether processed EEGs can reduce anesthetic delivery, improving the speed and quality of recovery, and reduce costs. In meta- analyses, titrating anesthesia to achieve BIS values of 40 to 60 resulted in small (2–4 minutes) reductions in awakening times and recovery room stay (4–8 minutes), but failed to facilitate earlier home discharge.<sup>399,400</sup>

BIS titration also produced modest reductions in PONV (32% vs. 38%),<sup>399</sup> and cost savings were substantially less than the disposable costs associated with BIS.<sup>399</sup> Compared to BIS, auditory evoked potential monitoring achieved similar small reductions in drug usage and awakening times,<sup>401,402</sup> with unchanged<sup>401</sup> or shortened discharge times.<sup>402</sup> Spectral entropy monitoring also achieved only small reductions in drug use and recovery times, although the quality of evidence was rather poor.<sup>403</sup>

Concerns have been raised when using processed EEGs to optimize anesthetic delivery. Although anesthesia is usually adequate at values below 60, some patients may form memories at 40.<sup>404</sup> Thus, titrating anesthesia to BIS values of 40 to 50, to reduce costs and recovery times, may unintentionally increase the risk for awareness.<sup>405</sup> Conversely, the geriatric population is a growing proportion of ambulatory surgical patients, and acute delirium can delay or prevent ambulatory discharge. While our understanding of anesthesia’s possible impact on postoperative cognitive dysfunction (POCD) is still evolving,<sup>406,407</sup> a Cochrane review of almost 3000 patients concluded that anesthesia optimized by processed EEG indices or evoked potentials decreased both acute postoperative delirium and POCD incidence at three months.<sup>408</sup> Future studies are needed to determine if this translates into reductions in unplanned admissions or reduced recovery room times for the geriatric surgical outpatient. Although a lack of clear clinical or financial benefit exists from processed EEG it notably involves minimal clinical effort or patient risk. There is cost. As with any monitor, use does not prevent adverse events or improve patient care unless someone interprets the presented information and acts accordingly.<sup>409</sup>

### TARGET- CONTROLLED INHALATIONAL ANESTHESIA

End- tidal target- controlled inhalational anesthesia may also be used to monitor and maintain anesthetic depth. In 2022, the FDA approved the first software to semi- automate inhalational anesthetic delivery during surgery for use in the US (https://www.accessdata.fda.gov/cdrh_docs/pdf2 1/P210018B.pdf), although such systems have been sold in Europe for several years. The software, compatible with General Electric’s (GE’s) Aisys CS<sup>2</sup> Anesthesia Delivery System, allows anesthesia providers to preset the desired end- tidal concentrations of oxygen and volatile anesthetic agents. The system can then adjust to maintain these preset levels throughout surgery. Other systems on the market in Europe include FLOW- i (Maquet, Solna, Sweden) and Zeus (Dräger, Lübeck, Germany).

Publications report that end- tidal control may improve efficiency, reducing drug waste and decreasing the associated costs on both the health care system and environment.<sup>410,411</sup>

## Recovery from Ambulatory Anesthesia

Recovery is traditionally divided into three stages. Early, phase 1 recovery occurs in a PACU and entails further awakening and management of pain and nausea, with monitoring of hemodynamic stability. Intermediate

<!-- PDF page 22 -->

recovery continues in the phase 2 (step- down) recovery or in a separate ward area and ends when the patient achieves the criteria for home discharge (see later discussion). The phase 1 and phase 2 aspects of recovery may occur in separate locations or within the same room.

### EARLY RECOVERY

The recovery room or PACU should be centrally placed to all operating rooms and requires the same standard of staffing and equipment as are provided for inpatients.<sup>412</sup> The PACU may be shared with inpatients in some facilities, but recovery times can be shortened dramatically if there is a separated phase 1 PACU dedicated to ambulatory patients.<sup>11</sup> In the United States, the ratio of nurses to patients is usually lower than in the inpatient PACU, for ambulatory typically 1:3, reflecting the lower acuity of postprocedural needs.<sup>6</sup> Patient care should be adequately transferred to the PACU nursing staff, relaying preoperative and intraoperative problems and postoperative instructions. The nature and frequency of monitoring in the PACU is determined by the nature of surgery and the state of recovery. Because ambulatory anesthetics are typically short- acting, supplemental oxygen (O2) administration may be unnecessary in the PACU,<sup>413</sup> provided the patient’s saturation level of O2 in hemoglobin (SpO2) remains above 92% on air.

In the United Kingdom, patients may be discharged from phase 1 to phase 2 of recovery when they are awake and oriented, normothermic, able to maintain their own airway and ventilation, and demonstrate cardiovascular stability. Wounds should be reasonably dry, and pain and PONV should be minimal and adequately treated. This assessment is usually made on clinical judgment.<sup>412</sup> In the United States, transfer from phase 1 to phase 2 is commonly based on predefined, physician- determined criteria. Typical ambulatory criteria include being awake with stable vital signs, minimal pain, minimal nausea, and the ability to sit with minimal dizziness.<sup>414</sup> If more standardized data are desired, a scoring system can be used. The most commonly used system is the modified Aldrete score,<sup>415</sup> which assigns points on the basis of activity, ventilation, blood pressure, consciousness, and oxygenation (Table 68.5). Length of PACU stay is one of the key end points, along with awakening, orientation, and extubation times, used to evaluate early recovery in ambulatory anesthesia research.

### SECOND- STAGE RECOVERY

Second- stage recovery prepares patients for leaving the ambulatory surgery unit and taking over their own care. Patients should sit upright on beds or reclining chairs as an aid to mobilization. After low- dose spinal anesthesia, mobilization is usually possible within an hour of the return of full motor function, or about 2.5 to 3 hours after the start of spinal anesthesia.<sup>177,416</sup>

### FAST- TRACK RECOVERY

With the increased use of short- acting drugs and techniques, many patients will have already met the discharge criteria before, or by the time, they reach the PACU.<sup>417</sup> If this is the case, admission to the PACU will only generate

unnecessary delay while further observations are performed. Instead, these patients may bypass phase 1 recovery and go directly to the phase 2 unit; this is known as fast- track recovery.

The modified Aldrete score can also be used to assess fast- track eligibility.<sup>418</sup> Because this score does not assess pain or PONV, which are traditionally treated in the PACU, White and Song<sup>419</sup> added two additional categories to derive their fast- track recovery score. Although using this score reduced the proportion of patients who met the fasttrack criteria on PACU arrival, it also significantly reduced the number of patients who required parenteral analgesia or antiemetics in the step- down area.<sup>419</sup>

The criteria for transfer from phase 1 to phase 2 and the criteria for direct entry to phase 2 should be the same. Fast- track recovery is the norm for patients who have had local anesthesia, but it is also appropriate for many patients receiving sedation<sup>420,421</sup> and low- dose spinal anesthesia.<sup>177</sup> Patients receiving general anesthesia also may be able to undergo fast- track recovery, which is appealing because improved recovery provides the patient with a higherquality experience, enabling them to return toward normal in a more pleasant, comfortable, and facilitative environment. It also frees up the more intensive resources of phase 1 recovery for those patients who need them.

Accomplishing fast- track recovery is complex. In one facility, fast- track recovery was only achieved in just over 60% of those who met the PACU bypass criteria.<sup>422</sup> The use of depth of anesthesia monitoring has been claimed to facilitate fast- track recovery,<sup>423</sup> whereas others have not found it advantageous.<sup>424</sup> Accomplishing fast- track recovery requires not only anesthesia recovery readiness, but also the support of a facility- based process, including nursing and surgeon participation and environment support.

The economic case for fast- track recovery should be considered separately.<sup>425</sup> In some cases, fast- track recovery has shortened overall recovery stay, comparable to,<sup>426</sup> or even



::: details 原始表格與符號（PDF 第 22 頁）

![第 68 章，原始 PDF 第 22 頁](assets/chapter-68/source-page-0022.png)

:::

<!-- PDF page 23 -->

longer than,<sup>420</sup> the time that would have been spent in the PACU. However, nursing workload was not reduced,<sup>426</sup> and others have found no difference in overall recovery time.<sup>422</sup> Nurses in the step- down unit are not always readily available to receive patients and often report patients arriving cold or without all the fast- track criteria actually being met.<sup>427</sup> Although fast- track recovery appears financially beneficial, actual savings will be made only if the PACU is not needed at all or if staffing levels can be reduced, which is not supported by the evidence to date.<sup>425</sup> Nursing workload and costs may simply be shifted from one area to another, with no overall savings.<sup>426</sup> Fast- track recovery may still help to improve patient flow and may work best in small units that use staff flexibly between different areas.<sup>425</sup> But the most productive approach may be to enable the fastest possible path for all patients through their recovery to discharge home.

## Postoperative Pain

The management of postoperative pain should begin well before the patient undergoes surgery. Patients need to have appropriate expectations about what they are likely to experience during their recovery.<sup>428</sup> At preoperative assessment, patients should be provided with information about the likely extent and duration of pain after surgery. They should also be advised about simple measures to reduce pain, including advice to rest in a comfortable position, raising swollen limbs, use of heat or cold packs, and the benefits of distraction. Prevention is the mainstay of pain management, yet studies have shown that pain management after ambulatory surgery is often inadequate.<sup>429–431</sup> Common causes are a lack of adherence to analgesic guidelines and a failure to provide multimodal analgesia.<sup>431</sup> Too often there is over- reliance on opioid analgesia, resulting in predictable adverse effects,<sup>432</sup> that are second only to inadequate pain relief in causing unnecessary hospital admission.<sup>429</sup>

### MULTIMODAL ANALGESIA

Multimodal analgesia relies on the additive or synergistic combination of drugs acting at various points on the pain pathway.<sup>433</sup> Typical combinations include local anesthetic wound infiltration or regional techniques and routine NSAIDs, with small doses of opioids added as needed. Topical therapies may also be of some benefit, with both lidocaine and glyceryl trinitrate patches found to provide effective topical analgesia after a variety of ambulatory procedures.<sup>434</sup> Multimodal regimens have been shown to be effective after several ambulatory surgical procedures.<sup>435,436</sup> An opioid- sparing effect exists for several drug combinations,<sup>437</sup> but most evidence is limited to an opioid plus one other drug, with little evaluation of true multimodal analgesia or attempts to identify optimal combinations.<sup>431</sup> Analgesic efficacy appears to differ with the nature of surgery,<sup>438</sup> suggesting that multimodal analgesic regimens should be specifically tailored.<sup>439</sup> Nevertheless, reducing the opioid dose does decrease the incidence of PONV to a corresponding degree and may also reduce other opioid adverse effects, such as sedation, sleep disturbances, urinary retention, and respiratory depression.<sup>432</sup> As yet, no evidence indicates that multimodal analgesia improves

long- term patient outcome<sup>440</sup> because of the small number of subjects studied and the infrequent incidence of adverse outcomes after ambulatory surgery.

### Acetaminophen

Often considered a weak analgesic, acetaminophen has equivalent and sustained effects compared with other perioperative analgesics.<sup>441</sup> Acetaminophen may be given orally, rectally, or via an intravenous route. Studies regarding the opioid- sparing effects of acetaminophen are inconsistent.<sup>442</sup> A large retrospective study and several RCTs showed no such benefit<sup>443–445</sup> while other RCTs and meta- analyses demonstrated a reduction in opioid consumption.<sup>446–448</sup> Since these studies were conducted in different surgical populations using different protocols, it is challenging to compare them. When balancing the relatively high safety profile of acetaminophen and the evidence of an opioid- sparing effect in some studies, it seems reasonable to continue the use of acetaminophen as an adjuvant analgesic.

### NSAIDs

NSAIDs encompass anti- inflammatory, antipyretic, antithrombotic, and analgesic qualities. NSAIDs and COX- 2 inhibitors are part of multimodal analgesia throughout the perioperative period in the ambulatory patient. Nonselective NSAIDs (naproxen, ibuprofen, diclofenac) inhibit both COX- 1 and COX- 2. Selective NSAIDs (celecoxib, lumiracoxib) inhibit mainly COX- 2.

NSAIDs may be given orally, rectally, intramuscular, and via the intravenous route. Recently, several meta- analyses evaluated the efficacy and safety of single- dose intravenous NSAIDs for acute postoperative pain. Diclofenac and ketorolac were found to give good pain relief, but not enough data was found to support or refute the use of IV ibuprofen.<sup>449–451</sup>

Dipyrone is an NSAID banned in some countries and used in others. It gives good pain relief, but the risk of lifethreatening blood disorders makes its use questionable.<sup>452</sup>

NSAIDs should be used with caution, or their use may even be restricted in patients with active peptic ulcer disease, increased risk of bleeding, cardiovascular disease, chronic kidney, and chronic liver disease.

Concerns regarding the use of NSAIDs and a possible deleterious effect on bone healing have been raised in the past. A meta- analysis of observational studies found no such correlation when only high- quality studies were analyzed.<sup>453</sup> NSAIDs may cause a transient reduction in the creatinine clearance on the first postoperative day but with no significant long- term effect.<sup>454</sup> A recent meta- analysis found no correlation between the perioperative use of NSAIDs and operative bleeding.<sup>455</sup>

### Gabapentinoids

Gabapentinoids were initially marketed as anticonvulsants but are also effective for chronic neuropathic pain. In the past couple of decades gabapentinoids gained popularity as a treatment for acute postoperative pain. However, their routine use can cause neurologic and respiratory adverse reactions. In the context of the ambulatory patient, these adverse events can delay discharge.<sup>456</sup> A meta- analysis evaluated the influence of gabapentinoids on postoperative pain at 6, 12, 24, 48, and 72 hours. No clinically significant difference in postoperative

<!-- PDF page 24 -->

acute, subacute, and chronic pain was observed. Gabapentinoids were associated with a lower risk of PONV, but with an increased length of stay in the hospital and increased risk of dizziness and visual disturbance.<sup>457</sup> Thus, the routine use of gabapentinoids as perioperative analgesics is not justified.

### Dexamethasone

Dexamethasone may be used as part of multimodal analgesia in higher doses than when administered as an antiemetic. The onset of action of dexamethasone is 1–2 hours, hence the recommendation to administer it prior to surgical trauma. A meta- analysis found a small but significant advantage in the use of dexamethasone as an analgesic. Patients treated with dexamethasone experienced less postoperative pain, required fewer postoperative opioids, and had shorter PACU stays. Twenty- four hours after the operation, patients that received dexamethasone had higher blood glucose levels, but there was no increase in wound infection or delayed healing.<sup>458</sup>

Another meta- analysis examined the effect of single- dose dexamethasone in abdominal surgery. Single- dose dexamethasone effectively reduced pain scores, was more effective when given preoperatively, and was most effective when administered in an intermediate dose (6.4–10 mg). No difference in PACU length of stay was found, but that can be attributed to the population investigated (abdominal surgery) and may not be relevant to the ambulatory patient.<sup>459</sup>

The frequent use of dexamethasone has raised concerns regarding surgical site infections. In a recent large RCT, 8 mg of intraoperative dexamethasone did not increase the incidence of surgical-site infections.<sup>460</sup>

### RESCUE ANALGESIA

Despite prophylactic measures, some patients will experience pain on awakening after surgery. Milder cases may be

amenable to treatment with additional oral analgesia, but more severe pain will usually require parenteral opioids. Long- acting parenteral opioids are rarely indicated. Fentanyl is commonly used for this purpose, and small boluses (20– 25 mcg) rapidly achieve analgesia. Compared to morphine, fentanyl results in more rapid control of pain and reduces the occurrence of PONV.<sup>461</sup> Rescue fentanyl also produced fewer adverse effects than oxycodone.<sup>462</sup> Administration of additional oral analgesia as soon as the pain is controlled will usually prevent a recurrence at a later stage of recovery. During the recovery period, pain should be assessed on a regular basis and treated according to protocols (Fig. 68.4).

### PAIN MANAGEMENT AT HOME

In the United States, patients are often given prescriptions for postoperative analgesics, including weak opioids, preferably before surgery so the medications will be at home when the patient needs them. In the United Kingdom, patients may be given standardized take- home analgesic packs that can be dispensed from the ambulatory surgery unit, avoiding pharmacy delays. These should be used when necessary for pain not adequately controlled with acetaminophen and NSAIDs.

### Managing Opioid Complications

Although multimodal techniques aim to minimize opioid use, strong opioids can be necessary as a rescue option after more invasive surgery. Analgesics such as morphine and oxycodone provide more intense and prolonged effects, but are associated with more intense and prolonged typical opioid side effects. Novel delivery techniques, such as iontophoretic<sup>463</sup> or nasal<sup>464</sup> fentanyl, or sublingual sufentanil,<sup>465</sup> may improve patient convenience but do nothing to reduce adverse effects. In addition to multimodal analgesia, attempts to limit these adverse effects have included the development of drugs such as tramadol, which combine

In: Smith I, McWhinnie D, Jackson I, eds. _Day Case Surgery_ . London: Oxford University Press; 2012:133–145. With permission.)

<!-- PDF page 25 -->

opioid and nonopioid mechanisms of action. Tramadol is effective after ambulatory surgery,<sup>466,467</sup> but it is still associated with a frequent incidence of unwanted effects, including sedation, dizziness, and particularly PONV.<sup>468</sup> Tapentadol is licensed in both the United States and the United Kingdom. It has a dual action similar to that of tramadol, with comparable efficacy to oxycodone but fewer GI adverse effects, such as nausea, vomiting, and constipation.<sup>469</sup> Unlike tramadol, it does not require metabolic activation or suffer from isomer- dependent pharmacodynamics.<sup>171</sup>

Combining oral oxycodone with naloxone (Targinact) antagonizes the GI effects, in particular preventing opioidinduced constipation, but has little effect on analgesia because first- pass metabolism prevents much naloxone from reaching the central nervous system.<sup>171</sup> Alvimopan is another peripherally acting μ- opioid (MOP) receptor antagonist intended to reduce opioid- induced constipation. Since peripheral MOP receptors also may partially mediate opioidassociated PONV, delayed gastric emptying, and urinary retention,<sup>171</sup> these drugs offer the hope that opioid analgesia without serious adverse effects may yet be a possibility.

### Local Anesthetic Administration at Home

Sending patients home with perineural or incisional catheters is an increasingly utilized option for postoperative pain management.<sup>470</sup> Patient- controlled regional anesthesia (PCRA) with an elastomeric balloon pump has provided effective analgesia and reduced pain intensity during mobilization when ropivacaine is delivered into a perioperatively placed wound catheter after subacromial decompression.<sup>471</sup> Notably, most local anesthetics, including bupivacaine, lidocaine, and ropivacaine, are chondrotoxic to human articular cartilage _in vitro_ , and the risk may be increased with longer exposure to higher concentrations of local anesthetic, such as with a pain pump, compared to that associated with a single injection.<sup>472</sup> As analysis of evidence indicated a causal relationship between intraarticular local anesthetic infusions and chondrolysis,<sup>473</sup> the use of intra- articular catheters has largely ceased after shoulder and knee surgery.

Excellent analgesia with continuous interscalene block continued at home can allow and expedite same- day discharge after a variety of shoulder operations, including open rotator cuff repair, subacromial decompression, and joint replacement.<sup>474</sup>

A variety of infusion pump designs are available for local anesthesia infusions or PCRA after discharge. Technical problems have been found with electronic pumps,<sup>475</sup> which seem to be unreliable for home use.<sup>474</sup> Patient satisfaction<sup>475</sup> has been reported with disposable elastomeric pumps, which are more reliable, although not all types function equally well and _in vivo_ performance does not necessarily match results obtained _in vitro_ .<sup>476</sup>

## Postoperative Nausea and Vomiting

PONV occurs in as many as 30% of a general surgical inpatient population given no antiemetics.<sup>477</sup> Some patients undergoing day surgery have a much smaller risk for symptoms, cited as less than 5%<sup>60,478</sup> for very minor surgery,

with reduced requirement for intraoperative and postoperative opioids. However, in the same studies, other ambulatory surgery patients had a predischarge incidence of PONV as high as 41%. When the potential occurrence of emetic sequelae after discharge is included in the estimation of overall risk, some authors cite an overall incidence of more than 40%, even after receiving an antiemetic.<sup>479</sup>

### RISK ASSESSMENT AND STRATEGIES

Some have recommended that the management of PONV for ambulatory patients should involve universal multimodal pharmacologic prophylaxis to minimize the symptoms that might delay timely discharge or subsequent recurrence in the home environment.<sup>480</sup> However, current recommendations<sup>481</sup> advocate a more focused approach toward the use of preventive pharmacotherapy, having first minimized baseline risk resulting from emetogenic stimuli, such as adequate hydration, the use of local–regional techniques, or if a general anesthetic is needed, reducing exposure to volatile anesthetics, N2O, and opioid- based analgesia. Avoiding neostigmine was previously advocated, but may not be beneficial.<sup>482</sup> Individual patient factors should then be identified to stratify relative risk. The scoring system originally derived from a cohort of ear, nose, and throat patients by Apfel and colleagues in 1998<sup>483</sup> is popular because of its relative simplicity; yet debate continues regarding discriminating power<sup>480</sup> and its applicability to ambulatory surgery patients, because the predicted risk for PONV (Table 68.6) seems to be overestimated in this population, presumably as a result of the original scoring system having been developed for inpatients. This seems to be confirmed in a subsequent paper by the same author,<sup>479</sup> where PACU- only rates of nausea were 19.9%, vomiting 3.9%, and nausea and/or vomiting 20.7% across an ambulatory patient cohort with characteristics that would seem to predict greater risk from the original algorithm. However, in the same study, postdischarge rates of PONV until the second postoperative day were higher: 36.6% nausea, 11.9% vomiting, 13.3% severe nausea, and 5.0% severe vomiting. From these data, the authors developed a predictive scoring

:::

<!-- PDF page 26 -->

algorithm for postdischarge nausea and vomiting (PDNV) (Table 68.7). Other scoring algorithms exist for prediction of risk for PONV in the ambulatory environment, but their relative complexity mandates the use of a calculator or computer.<sup>478</sup> A reevaluation of risk factors for PONV<sup>484</sup> reiterated the importance of those characteristics included in the original Apfel score,<sup>477</sup> but this algorithm included younger age and duration but not type of surgery. A simple policy whereby two antiemetic interventions were administered to all male patients and three to all females<sup>485</sup> resulted in better compliance and greater effectiveness than several previously published strategies,<sup>486</sup> although at the risk of exposing more patients to the potential side effects of unnecessary antiemetics.

Antiemetic prophylaxis should therefore be tailored to assessment of likely risk for symptoms with the use of two agents for patients with 1–2 risk factors and 3–4 agents for those with >2 risk factors, using drugs with different modes of action.<sup>481</sup> The IMPACT study<sup>207</sup> demonstrated that ondansetron 4 mg, droperidol 1.25 mg, and dexamethasone 4 mg were equally effective in reducing the risk for PONV by an order of 25% and that using these three drugs together would be approximately additive. Specifically, the prophylactic use of one of these drugs decreases the estimated risk for PONV from 60% to 44%; use of two drugs would further decrease the incidence from 44% to 33% and three drugs to 24%. The use of TIVA with propofol with the concomitant avoidance of N2O had similar equivalence to the use of one antiemetic.<sup>207</sup> However, the antiemetic effectiveness of TIVA is time- limited and patients receiving TIVA appear to have a higher risk of late PONV, starting 2 to 6 hours after surgery.<sup>487</sup> Simple measures, such as the routine administration of approximately 1 to 2 L of intravenous crystalloid fluids, reduce the incidence and severity of PONV, decrease dizziness and drowsiness,<sup>488</sup> and reduce postoperative pain in high- risk groups.<sup>489</sup> Allowing patients to drink clear liquids for as long as possible before surgery can also reduce PONV.<sup>132</sup>

### ANTIEMETIC AGENTS

### First- Generation Drugs

Metoclopramide is a dopaminergic (D2) and serotonergic (5- HT3, peripheral 5- HT4 at higher doses) antagonist with gastric prokinetic properties that was first described in 1964. A meta- analysis of a standard clinical dose of 10 mg showed little evidence of pharmacologic benefit for PONV,<sup>490</sup> but after a number of studies by Fujii were withdrawn, reanalysis suggests that there may be some benefit.<sup>491</sup> However, current guidelines still do not list metoclopramide for first- line treatment.<sup>482</sup> Higher doses, such as 20 to 25 mg, are more effective<sup>492</sup> but are associated with more akathisia.

Droperidol is a butyrophenone with an antiemetic effect resulting from dopaminergic receptor (D2) antagonism that received a black box warning for the FDA- approved dose of 2.5 mg<sup>493</sup> or greater because of potential QT interval prolongation. This and other side effects of droperidol, such as sedation and akathisia, were significant but at higher doses than are commonly used for PONV prophylaxis,<sup>493</sup> which are typically 1.25 mg or less. A meta- analysis<sup>494</sup> confirmed the antiemetic action of low- dose droperidol. Droperidol use is less in the United States since the black box warning was added but primarily for medicolegal reasons rather than because of concerns with efficacy or side effects.

Antagonists of the histamine H1 receptor have a particular efficacy in the management of nausea and vomiting precipitated by vestibular pathways, with a documented benefit in the management of travel sickness and surgical procedures for strabismus or involving the middle ear. Dimenhydrinate (a combination of diphenhydramine and 8- chlorotheophylline that was added to reduce drowsiness) has an antiemetic effectiveness deemed similar to that of both droperidol and 5- HT3 antagonists,<sup>495</sup> but potential adverse reactions from these antihistamines include marked sedation, dry mouth, urinary retention, and blurred vision as a result of associated muscarinic receptor antagonism. Meclizine, an H1- receptor antagonist and anti–motion sickness medication, is minimally sedating, long- acting, and effective for treatment of PONV and for prevention of PDNV.<sup>496</sup> In the United States, it is inexpensive and available without prescription, making it an attractive postdischarge choice. A transdermal scopolamine delivery system is also available. This patch was designed to deliver a total dose of 1 mg of scopolamine at a sustained, constant rate over a period of 3 days.<sup>497</sup> Numerous studies have shown transdermal scopolamine to be effective in reducing the incidence and severity of PONV and PDNV, with efficacy comparable to that of ondansetron or droperidol. It has a prolonged duration of over 24 hours, but the onset is delayed, becoming effective within 2 to 4 hours of applying the patch.<sup>498</sup> When applying the patch the night before surgery, the incidence of nausea and vomiting was reduced from 62.5% and 37.5% to 20.8% and 8.3%, respectively, compared to placebo in the first 24 hours after gynecologic laparoscopy.<sup>499</sup> In the United States, clinicians apply the patch preoperatively, knowing that onset of PDNV efficacy will be in the early recovery period for shorter surgery, but that it is one of the few drugs that continues to be effective significantly postdischarge.<sup>497</sup> Adverse effects, particularly dry mouth



::: details 原始表格與符號（PDF 第 26 頁）

![第 68 章，原始 PDF 第 26 頁](assets/chapter-68/source-page-0026.png)

:::

<!-- PDF page 27 -->

but also somnolence, dizziness, and blurred vision, are relatively common but minor.<sup>497,499</sup>

### Serotonin 5- HT3 Receptor Antagonists

The 5- HT3 antagonists have played a substantial role in the management of PONV since their introduction in the 1980s, because of their relatively benign adverse reaction profile compared with those of the then available drugs. Their prophylactic use is more effective when given just before the end of surgery.<sup>500</sup> They are effective drugs for emetic rescue therapy, with ondansetron seeming to have a more preferential effect on vomiting (number needed to treat [NNT] = 4) rather than nausea (NNT = 7).<sup>501</sup> Although relatively well tolerated, their side- effect profile includes an increased risk for headache (number needed to harm [NNH] = 36) and elevated liver enzymes (NNH = 31).<sup>501</sup> All serotonin subtype 3 antagonists have also been associated with QT interval prolongation. More recently released serotonin 5- HT3 receptor antagonists, such as palonosetron,<sup>502</sup> seem to exhibit similar characteristics with equal reduction of PONV rates in high- risk patients when used for prophylaxis, though the longer half- life (40 hours) may afford better protection against symptoms after discharge.<sup>503</sup> A large cost remains a significant barrier to use of newer nongeneric serotonin antagonists.

### Steroids

Dexamethasone is an effective antiemetic at intravenous doses of 4 to 8 mg.<sup>481,504</sup> This steroid may act centrally to either modulate the release of endorphins or inhibit prostaglandin synthesis. Given its delayed onset of action, dexamethasone should be administered as early as possible after induction of anesthesia.<sup>505</sup> Prophylactic dexamethasone is also effective in reducing postoperative pain and improving the quality of recovery,<sup>506,507</sup> although at somewhat higher doses (typically 8 mg).

### Neurokinin- 1 Antagonists

The role of tachykinins in the emetic pathway was first elucidated by immunohistologic studies identifying substance P in the dorsal vagal complex of the ferret, an area of the brain deemed essential in the vomiting reflex.<sup>508</sup> Subsequent research identified the potential value of specific antagonists to the neurokinin- 1 receptor, at which substance P and neurokinins A and B interact both centrally and peripherally in the gut to suppress vomiting. Aprepitant was the first commercially released drug of this class. A pooled analysis of trials comparing aprepitant to other commonly used antiemetics found that it is particularly superior in preventing postoperative vomiting.<sup>509</sup> Due to cost constraints, neurokinin- 1 antagonists may be reserved for patients at very high risk for PONV, or those in whom postoperative vomiting could be especially detrimental.

### MANAGING PERSISTENT NAUSEA AND VOMITING

The presence of continuing symptoms after antiemetic rescue therapy requires further clinical review. Consideration should be given to alternative causes for these symptoms, particularly hydration status, covert hypovolemia, or early infection. Review of vital signs (temperature, pulse, and

arterial blood pressure), allied with clinical examination to exclude an association with concomitant worsening of abdominal pain, potential septic foci, or urinary retention, is important to exclude more sinister causes, before consideration is given to symptomatic relief.

Administering 20 mL/kg of an isotonic electrolyte solution reduces the risk for nausea and dizziness after ambulatory surgery and volume repletion should be considered to attenuate continuing symptoms.<sup>488,510</sup> Ephedrine 0.5 mg/ kg intramuscularly is effective for prophylaxis and treatment, with efficacy comparable to that of droperidol and sedation scores lower than placebo.<sup>511</sup> No value exists in prescribing a previously used antiemetic drug within 6 hours of previous administration,<sup>482</sup> but other second- line drugs may be considered should first- line management fail. Some options include small- dose promethazine (6.25 mg); small- dose intravenous naloxone; propofol 20 mg; phenothiazines, including prochlorperazine, perphenazine, or a neurokinin antagonist. For patients who have not received prophylaxis, the 5- HT3 antagonists are the preferred first treatment of PONV and the class of drugs with best proven efficacy for treatment.<sup>482</sup>

## Enhanced Recovery after Surgery (ERAS)

Enhanced Recovery After Surgery (ERAS) are programs that standardize and optimize perioperative care and postoperative outcomes. ERAS was introduced at the beginning of the millennium, emphasizing the quality of the surgical end point rather than the speed of recovery, as was implicated by the term Fast Track Recovery. ERAS protocols address issues of systemic inflammation, catabolic response to the surgical insult, fluid hemostasis, anemia, PONV, and pain. Accordingly, ERAS includes several elements: a multidisciplinary team caring for the surgical patient and a multimodal approach addressing recovery- delaying issues and complications.

The ERAS Society (erassociety.org) publishes guidelines specific to different surgical specialties and disease- specific scenarios. However, many of these protocols are based on expert opinion in the absence of high- quality evidence.

The implementation of ERAS protocols may allow surgeries otherwise requiring hospitalization to be discharged on the same day.<sup>512</sup> Since ERAS was initially developed for the inpatient setting, translatability to the ambulatory setting is partial. However, ERAS protocols that were implemented in ambulatory centers show promise. In one example, ERAS implementation in an ambulatory surgery center led to a decrease in opioid consumption.<sup>513</sup> In a breast cancer surgical center, the implementation of an ERAS protocol led to a significant increase in sameday discharge (from 7.2% to 58.6%) and a decrease in the length of stay in those admitted (from 1.6 days to 1.2 days<sup>514</sup> ). A large retrospective study showed the impact of implementing an ERAS program in an ambulatory oncologic surgical center. They reported a decreased intraoperative opioid use and transition time to oral analgesics. The median time to first ambulation was 5–6 hours, the rate of patient readmissions within 30 days was 2.6%, and urgent care visits were 6.8%.<sup>515</sup>

<!-- PDF page 28 -->

In a randomized clinical trial, 108 patients admitted for acute uncomplicated appendectomy were divided into an ERAS group and a conventional care group. The ERAS group was discharged after a mean of 9.7 hours (compared with 23.2 hours in the conventional care group) and had a significantly reduced time to resume diet and less moderate to severe postoperative pain. The rate of readmissions was similar.<sup>516</sup>

In several studies on ambulatory patients, different modified ERAS protocols were used. The framework for ambulatory ERAS is opioid minimization pre- , intra- , and postoperatively. The common features of these protocols are the following:

Preoperative:

- Adequate outpatient support, education, and communication

- Feasible postoperative support and communication (telemedicine, phone calls, home visits)

- No major comorbidities

Intraoperative:

- Pre- emptive and intraoperative opioid- sparing multimodal analgesia

- Peripheral nerve blocks

- Local anesthetic wound infiltration by the surgeons

- Preferential use of regional anesthesia and/or sedation over general anesthesia when possible

- PONV prophylaxis pre- and intraoperative

Postoperative:

- Resume a full diet as soon as the patient is fit

- Early mobilization

The readmission rates were low across studies. Failure to void remains a problem delaying discharge, although ERAS protocols may decrease the incidence of urinary retention.<sup>515,517–520</sup> In conclusion, although ERAS was not initially designed for ambulatory centers, a modified ambulatory ERAS protocol can be well suited for the outpatient.

## Special Areas

### OFFICE- BASED PRACTICE

Office- based anesthesia is a form of ambulatory anesthesia that is rapidly expanding in North America and some parts of Europe. Arguably, the first ambulatory surgery center in the United States (the Downtown Anesthesia Clinic in Sioux City, Iowa) was an office- based practice.<sup>2</sup> Simple, minimally invasive surgical procedures have been performed in physicians’ offices for many years, using either local anesthesia or sedation. There is now a growing involvement of anesthesiologists, especially as the complexity of office- based surgery increases.

Advantages of office- based surgery include improved convenience for the patient, but the primary driver has been more control over scheduling and the work environment for the surgeon. The potential exists for significant profit going directly to the surgeon, but in addition, the lower overhead costs in this setting result in significantly lower overall costs for the procedure. For example, the total cost of a laparoscopic inguinal hernia repair was three and a half times

greater when performed in a hospital setting compared with an office facility.<sup>521</sup> The costs of open hernia repair<sup>521</sup> and various rhinology procedures<sup>522</sup> were approximately 2.5 times less when performed in the office. In the United States, office- based surgery covers a wide range of procedures.

However, legitimate concerns have been raised about the safety of office- based surgery. One comparative study showed a more than 10- fold increase in adverse incidents and deaths for procedures performed in a physician’s office compared with an ambulatory surgical center.<sup>523</sup> These disasters are often the result of unqualified or untrained individuals administering sedation in unsuitable or unaccredited facilities.<sup>523,524</sup> Deep sedation appears to be a considerable risk factor. From the ASA closed claims database, 40% of the deaths related to MAC occurred during facial and eye surgery, which are commonly performed in the office setting.<sup>343</sup> Hypoxia and hypoventilation resulting from oversedation was the most common cause of death, with poor vigilance, inadequate monitoring, and delayed resuscitation contributing to the deaths, half of which were judged to be preventable.<sup>343</sup> The common perception that sedation is safer than general anesthesia is not supported by these data.

In the United States, the regulation of office facilities is the responsibility of individual states, with nearly 30 states having some degree of oversight as of 2014.<sup>525</sup> Regulation does appear to improve safety, because no deaths were reported in a series of more than 23,000 cases from a fully accredited office staffed by board- certified anesthesiologists and surgeons.<sup>526</sup> In addition, safety appears to be improving in parallel with greater regulation.<sup>525</sup> Guidance on office- based anesthesia is available from the ASA and SAMBA,<sup>527</sup> and comprehensive recommendations from other organizations and experts have been published. Typical recommendations for safe office- based anesthesia are summarized in Box 68.2.

### Box 68.2 A Summary of Office- Based Surgery Practice Guidelines Collated from US Regulatory Bodies

Employment of appropriately trained and credentialed anesthesia personnel

Availability of properly maintained anesthesia equipment appropriate to the anesthesia care being provided

As complete documentation of the care provided as that required at other surgical sites

- Use of standard monitoring equipment according to the American Society of Anesthesiologists policies and guidelines

- Provision of a postanesthesia care unit or recovery area that

- is staffed by appropriately trained nursing personnel and provision of specific discharge instructions

- Availability of emergency equipment (e.g., airway equipment, cardiac resuscitation)

- Establishment of a written plan for emergency transport of patients to a site that provides more comprehensive care should an untoward event or complication occur that requires more extensive monitoring or overnight admission of the patient

Maintenance and documentation of a quality assurance program Establishment of a continuing education program for physicians and other facility personnel

- Safety standards that cannot be jeopardized for patient convenience or cost savings

<!-- PDF page 29 -->

In essence, the office setting must adhere to the same standards of care required in a hospital- based or freestanding ambulatory surgery facility. Strong safety processes must be in place, because the isolated office- based environment means that outside help is not immediately available.

The selection of patients for office- based anesthesia should adhere to robust guidelines for safe anesthetic care. Because perioperative complications are harder to manage in an isolated environment, selection criteria may need to be more restrictive than those currently advocated for ambulatory surgery in the hospital setting.<sup>48</sup> The selection process must include a matrix of procedure invasiveness, patient complexity, and the capabilities and comfort levels of the facility and its personnel.<sup>527</sup> Preoperative preparation should be guided by the same clinical acumen and common sense that drives the decision- making process at freestanding surgery centers, with any coexisting diseases under good control. Anesthetic techniques suitable for office- based surgical procedures are similar to those used for hospital- based and freestanding ambulatory surgery procedures. MAC remains common, although clearly a great challenge exists in “the judicious and skillful use of MAC anesthesia to achieve adequate sedation and analgesia for increasingly invasive procedures.”<sup>528</sup> Increasingly a move toward light general anesthesia is occurring – that is, using an SGA or face mask for airway management.<sup>529,530</sup> Propofol, sevoflurane, and desflurane are all suitable within the office setting, although an anesthesia machine is required to deliver volatile anesthetics. Standard equipment can be installed in frequently used offices, and portable equipment has been developed for less frequent use.<sup>524</sup> Fast- track recovery is the ideal with patients who are awake, alert, and able to transfer themselves from operating room table to a reclining chair, thus facilitating room turnover in approximately 10 minutes and home discharge within an hour of awakening.<sup>524</sup> It is important to minimize PDNV.<sup>531</sup> Recommendations have been made for other outcome measures that should routinely be audited after office- based anesthesia.<sup>527,532</sup> The American Medical Association and American College of Surgeons issued a set of Office- Based Surgery Core Principles to promote safety and quality of health care services for office procedures requiring sedation and anesthesia.<sup>533</sup> In the United Kingdom, a similar practice of office- based anesthesia for dental procedures was in place for several decades. A series of anesthetic deaths in dental offices led to several reviews, culminating in recommendations that all anesthetics be administered by accredited anesthesiologists with specific training and experience in dental anesthesia, as well as recommendations regarding resuscitation equipment and the availability of drugs needed for emergency use.<sup>534</sup> Partly because of the high costs of equipment and maintenance, the net result was the movement of all anesthesia out of the dental office and back into the hospital environment.<sup>535</sup> Subsequently, office- based anesthesia has never developed in the United Kingdom. Minor surgery under local anesthesia is performed in some specially equipped primary care surgery centers, whereas the majority of procedures that are performed in offices in the United States are managed in hospital- affiliated day surgery units, procedure rooms, or surgical outpatient clinics in the United Kingdom.

### ANESTHESIA IN NON- OPERATING- ROOMS AND REMOTE LOCATIONS

Many surgical conditions that formerly needed treatment in a hospital outpatient operating room are now being managed by interventional procedures done by non- surgeon physicians in radiology, cardiology, and endoscopy (see also Chapter 69). In many cases, deep sedation or anesthesia is still required, meaning the anesthesiologist has to move into unfamiliar and often hazardous environments. Collectively, they are sometimes called NORA (non- OR anesthesia). The problems associated with these diverse locations are considered in detail in Chapter 69, but because many of the procedures are performed on an ambulatory basis or require anesthetic management according to all of the usual short- stay principles, a brief description is required here. The basic sedative and anesthetic techniques already described are suitable for most cases, but the conduct of anesthesia is likely to have to be modified according to the specific environment.

Administration of anesthesia or sedation at remote locations is associated with significant risk.<sup>536</sup> Some of these risks are specific to particular areas (Table 68.8) but commonly

<!-- PDF page 30 -->

include an unfamiliar environment; small, cramped, or dark rooms; restricted patient access; inadequate or poorly trained support; restrictions on patient monitoring; and inadequate resources. An analysis of ASA closed claims arising from anesthesia care in remote locations revealed that adverse events had a higher probability of death than those arising in the operating room<sup>537</sup> and were primarily caused by an adverse respiratory event (44%). MAC was the most common anesthetic technique, and respiratory depression secondary to oversedation accounted for over a third of the claims. Care that was substandard and preventable by better monitoring was implicated in the majority of claims associated with death.<sup>537</sup> Many of these anesthetics appear to be intravenous general anesthesia without airway devices. In the United States, the CMS mandates that the chiefs of anesthesia services monitor and evaluate sedation practices throughout the hospital, and accrediting organizations, such as TJC and DNV, audit for compliance.

## Home Readiness and Beyond

### PATIENT INFORMATION

Before discharge, patients should receive postoperative instructions concerning aftercare, their transition to normal activities, and any requirements for follow- up. These instructions should be in writing<sup>538</sup> because retention of information is impaired during early recovery from anesthesia,<sup>539</sup> and ideally they should be repeated to the patient’s escort. Besides general advice, discharge information should include the early warning signs for the main complications of the specific procedure the patient has undergone<sup>538</sup> and the action to take should these occur.

### CRITERIA FOR DISCHARGE

Discharge of the patient after ambulatory surgery and anesthesia is a physician responsibility in the United States,<sup>10</sup> fulfilled by physician- written discharge criteria (Box 68.3). Typically, patients should be oriented and comfortable with cardiovascular stability (including when standing) and problem- free wounds. Alternatively, discharge criteria may be incorporated into a scoring system, such as the Postanesthetic Discharge Scoring System (Box 68.4).<sup>540</sup> Voiding urine is no longer considered an essential prerequisite for

### Box 68.3 Criteria for Facility Discharge Used at the Brigham and Women’s Hospital, United States

discharge in those at low risk for urinary retention, even after spinal anesthesia.<sup>541</sup> For patients at higher risk, the bladder volume should be assessed by ultrasound scanning,<sup>542</sup> and the patient managed accordingly (Fig. 68.5). Actual discharge is frequently nurse- managed,<sup>538,543</sup> provided patients have been managed according to an appropriate protocol and have met all the necessary discharge criteria.

No rationale exists for a minimum observation period after ambulatory surgery in most cases, although patients clearly must be observed for a sufficient period to ensure cardiovascular stability. One possible exception is tonsillectomy, in which a minimum observation period of 6 to 8 hours has been advocated to detect most primary hemorrhages.<sup>30</sup> However, even this has been challenged, with a 4- hour observation period<sup>544,545</sup> or less<sup>546</sup> regarded as safe.

### AFTERCARE AND FOLLOW- UP

Appropriate aftercare is one of the main safety features of ambulatory surgery. Acute complications may be related to anesthesia or surgery, and patients should receive careful predischarge education about what may occur for both. All patients should be provided with a 24- hour telephone number for emergency contact. This is often the ambulatory surgery unit during the daytime, but it may be necessary to provide another number or better still to automatically transfer calls when the unit closes at night.<sup>538</sup> In the United States, patients are also routinely given their surgeon’s office and answering service number to contact directly for surgical issues. If the patient’s call to the facility or physician does raise concerns, it is important that the patient be

brought back for early surgical review; going to the emergency department also can introduce unnecessary delay. Because the ambulatory patient is self- caring and likely to mobilize early, symptoms of surgical complications are noticed and reported sooner than if the patient were recovering in the hospital environment, allowing earlier detection and increased safety. Daily assessment of various recovery parameters at home using a smartphone- based app was popular with patients and also appeared to improve quality of recovery, probably by providing additional reassurance.<sup>547</sup> The system could also be used to request contact from a nurse and was found to be more cost- effective than other forms of postdischarge contact,<sup>548</sup> although others found a simple telephone call, which would be cheaper still, was similarly effective.<sup>549</sup>

### Follow- Up and Outcome Measures

The ASA has developed a set of outcome indicators specifically for ambulatory and office- based surgery and anesthesia.<sup>550</sup> These include outcome events of interest for days 1, 14, and 30 and ongoing continuous quality indicators. The IAAS<sup>551</sup> has developed a series of indicators (Table 68.9) useful in the evaluation of the overall success of organizational performance, and these mirror the advice from other

national specialist societies.<sup>48,552</sup> Lemos and Barros<sup>553</sup> further defined the value of outcome reporting in a range of domains subdivided into clinical, organizational, social, and economic factors that allows the reporting of both individual and institutional performance (Table 68.10). Returnmail questionnaires can be used for patient follow- up after ambulatory surgery to help identify common sequelae that ambulatory patients should realistically expect to experience.<sup>428</sup> No matter how data are collected, it is important that information from quality indicators is fed back in an effective way to individual clinicians and clinical units to support continuous improvement.<sup>554</sup>

### Adverse Effects After Ambulatory Surgery

Minor adverse events are common after ambulatory surgery and anesthesia (86%).<sup>428</sup> Drowsiness is the most common effect persisting after discharge (62%), and aches and sore throat are common in intubated patients (47% and 49%, respectively). Headache (25%) and dizziness (20%) also occur, but nausea and vomiting after discharge are less common (17% and 7%, respectively). Patients may take 2 to 3 days before they are able to resume their usual activities.<sup>428</sup> Information about these known side effects should be incorporated into the preoperative patient education

of accurate information about the expected perioperative process is important; other factors that lead to higher satisfaction are effective postoperative analgesia, minimal emetic sequelae, staff courtesy and privacy in the unit, short waiting times before surgery, no perception of being rushed, postoperative telephone contact, and naturally, good surgical outcome.<sup>551</sup>

Acute cardiovascular events (hypertension and hypotension, dysrhythmias, cardiac ischemia, and arrest) occur with an overall frequency of 2.9%, with greater risk in patients with preexisting cardiovascular morbidity; respiratory events (hypoxemia, laryngospasm, bronchospasm, aspiration, pulmonary edema, and pneumothorax) occurred in 0.1% of the study population, with increased risk in smokers, asthmatics, and the obese.<sup>61</sup> Unanticipated overnight admission has a worldwide reported incidence of between 1% and 6%. Caution is required in the use of this indicator as a standard to achieve, however, unless the reasons for the admission are documented. Although the indicator may provide evidence of inadequate preoperative assessment and patient optimization, differences in surgical case mix and complexity may explain any variation across institutions. Ultraconservative selection criteria could result in an extremely low overnight admission rate and inappropriately give the impression of a highly performing unit rather than one that was excessively cautious with patient selection. Cross- comparison of this indicator with rates of ambulatory care as a proportion of all elective surgery, either by specialty or specific operation, could assist with interpretation.

Patient satisfaction is a difficult metric to define, depending somewhat on the patient’s expectation of care. Nevertheless, patient satisfaction is generally very high after ambulatory surgery.
